# SlopOS — architecture and engineering audit

*2026-07-30. Audited at commit `5c05530b` on `develop`.*

## How this was produced

Six web-research briefs on proven kernel designs (Linux, FreeBSD, seL4, Fuchsia/Zircon,
Asterinas, Theseus, Windows NT, illumos, Redox, Hubris) → two repo-wide recon sweeps
(crate topology; duplication and drift) → fifteen deep per-subsystem auditors, each
grounded in the briefs → 107 adversarial refuters, one per critical/high finding and two
independent lenses per critical one, each instructed to **default to REFUTED** → four
completeness critics hunting what a per-subsystem partition structurally cannot see.

| | count |
|---|---|
| Raw findings | 253 |
| Deduped critical/high sent to adversarial verification | 93 |
| Confirmed exactly as stated | 13 |
| Confirmed with corrections (real defect, claim overstated) | 79 |
| Refuted — a guard was found | 1 |
| Post-verification security severity | 11 high · 36 medium · 46 low |
| Medium/low findings not individually verified | 156 |
| Additional gaps from the completeness pass | 31 |

The 13:79 confirmed-to-corrected ratio is mostly **severity recalibration**, not error.
Auditors ranked by importance; refuters ranked by attacker-reachable impact, and a panic
inside a `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]` crate is denial of service, not memory corruption. Every
"corrected" finding is a real defect at a corrected severity. Read the two axes separately:
several findings that are *low* security severity are *high* engineering priority, because
they are false statements in the documentation that the project's own safety argument rests on.

Baseline facts measured directly, not taken from documentation: the suite is **2716/2716
green in 35.9 s** under KVM; TCB ratio by the project's own script is **0.53 %**; 114
syscalls; 43+ distinct error enums; **2 TODO markers tree-wide**.

## Verdict

This is one of the strongest hobby kernels in existence, and the parts that are good are
good for real reasons rather than by accident. It is also carrying a specific, coherent
class of debt: **the kernel has no process object, no credential, and no resource limit**,
and almost every serious finding below is a corollary of one of those three absences.

What is genuinely excellent, with receipts:

- **`Frame::drop` publishes the metadata slot as UNUSED before returning the page to the
  allocator** (`slopos-ostd/src/mm/frame.rs:1559-1579`), with the pre-fix ordering encoded
  as a counterexample in `verification/proofs/frame_refcount.rs`. This closes the exact
  free-before-reset race that produces the PathCorrupt bug class.
- **Type identity is a `TypeId` *value*, never a vtable address** (`frame.rs:165-181`,
  checked at `:681-690`), with the reason — release builds duplicate const-promoted vtables
  across crates — written into the doc comment and locked by a regression test. This is the
  most commonly-botched cross-crate identity pattern in Rust and it is handled correctly.
- **The `Zeroed`/`Uninit` frame typestate** (`frame.rs:518-558`) makes "reads as all-zero" a
  compile-time property with `alloc_uninit` as the sole opt-out. That is strictly stronger
  than Linux's `init_on_alloc=1`, which is a runtime flag with no type-level consequence.
- **`compile_fail` doctests enforce the no-reference discipline on `UFrame` and `IoMem`**
  (`uframe.rs:130-153`, `io_mem.rs:406-425`), so a future `impl Deref` is a CI failure.
  Asterinas states this invariant in prose; SlopOS mechanised it.
- **The TCP stack has zero unbounded parse sites.** An auditor walked every untrusted-packet
  parser — Ethernet, IPv4, TCP header and options, ICMP, ARP, DHCP, DNS name decompression,
  fragment reassembly — and every one is length-guarded, every loop advances monotonically,
  and DNS pointer-chasing has a follow cap. CUBIC + HyStart++ is a real implementation with
  an integer cube root, not a sketch. The OOO reassembly tracker structurally cannot be
  driven into the CVE-2019-11478 shape.
- **`mm/src/paging/page_table_defs.rs:202-259`** is the best-argued code in the tree: it
  explains why `&[u64; 512]` over a page-table frame is an exclusivity claim the hardware
  page walker does not honour, uses per-entry atomics instead, and keeps `set_entry_at`
  `pub(crate)` so no `forbid(unsafe_code)` consumer can write an arbitrary u64 into an
  HHDM-reachable frame.
- **`KArc` uses saturating CAS loops rather than `fetch_add`/`fetch_sub`**
  (`heap.rs:920-963`), because a plain `fetch_sub` racing a saturating increment could undo
  saturation. `alloc::sync::Arc` handles this by aborting; SlopOS handles it correctly.
- Two TODO markers in 340k LoC, and `verification/STATUS.md` is a genuine attempt at an
  honest negative-space record — it volunteers that the ring proofs exclude weak memory.
  That posture is why this audit could find sharp things: the project does not hide.

## The five things to fix first

Ordered by "what breaks a user today", not by CVSS.

### 1. The 256th process created since boot can never run

`create_process_vm` recycles process *slots* but draws `process_id` from a strictly
monotonic counter that is never bounded and never reused
(`mm/src/process_vm.rs:1541-1542`). `execute_task` refuses to dispatch any task whose
`process_id >= MAX_PROCESSES` (256) and terminates it
(`sched/src/scheduler.rs:1071-1084`). So the 256th process is created successfully, killed
at first dispatch with a klog line, and so is every process after it — permanently, until
reboot.

Run 255 commands in the shell and the machine can no longer start a program. A 36-second
test run cannot see this. Two independent verification lenses reached it from opposite
directions: one hunting the TLB consequence found the dispatch guard that neutralises it,
the other found that the guard *is* the bug.

**Fix.** Allocate `process_id` from a recycling ID allocator with a generation counter
(Linux `idr`/pid bitmap; FreeBSD `pfind` hash), or make every `MAX_PROCESSES`-sized array
slot-indexed rather than pid-indexed. The generation machinery to do this safely already
exists next door — `VmSlotAlloc::alloc_generation` at `process_vm.rs:119-124`.

### 2. Userland grants itself every privilege bit the kernel has

`core/src/syscall/context.rs:151` opens a section headed "Permission checks".
`is_compositor()`, `is_display_exclusive()` and `is_console_admin()` — documented at `:169`
as the `capable(CAP_SYS_TTY_CONFIG)` analogue — are the entirety of SlopOS's privilege
model, and all three read `task.flags`. `core/src/syscall/process_handlers.rs:225` copies
`attrs.flags` from user memory and hands it to `spawn_program_with_attrs` unmasked;
`core/src/exec/mod.rs:249` ORs in `TASK_FLAG_USER_MODE` and passes it to `task_build`.

Any process can spawn a child holding `TASK_FLAG_COMPOSITOR`, `TASK_FLAG_SYSTEM` or
`TASK_FLAG_DISPLAY_EXCLUSIVE`. It can also set `TASK_FLAG_NO_PREEMPT` (0x04), and
`sched/src/scheduler.rs:2350` returns early from the timer tick for such a task — a spin
loop in it owns its CPU permanently. Found independently by three auditors; verified by two
lenses; I confirmed it by hand.

Verification corrected two claims: `TASK_FLAG_KERNEL_MODE` *is* rejected downstream, and
`TASK_FLAG_SYSTEM` does *not* make a user task's panics fatal (that check lives in
`kernel_task_entry_shim`, which only kernel-mode tasks reach). The screen/input hijack and
the per-CPU lockup are real.

**Fix.** Mask `attrs.flags` against a `SPAWN_USER_SETTABLE` allow-list in
`syscall_spawn_path` before it reaches `spawn_program_with_attrs`. That is a two-line
change and it should land today. The real fix is a credential object, below.

### 3. `rt_sigreturn` hands userland a one-instruction machine halt

`rt_sigreturn` restores an XSAVE area read straight from the user stack with no validation
anywhere in the tree, and the resulting ring-0 `#GP` from `xrstor64` has no fixup entry — it
routes to `exception_fatal`. Both verification lenses confirmed it as stated and produced
the same three-line trigger: `mmap` a page, `memset` it to `0xFF`, point `rsp` at it, issue
syscall 105. No signal need ever have been delivered.

Adjacent, same class: `rt_sigaction` validates the signal number against 64 and then indexes
a 32-entry array, so any `signum` in `33..=64` is an out-of-bounds panic from unprivileged
userland.

**Fix.** Validate the XSAVE header before `xrstor` (Linux does this in
`copy_user_to_xstate` — reject reserved bits, unsupported components, bad MXCSR), and add
an exception-table fixup so a `#GP` in that window becomes `SIGSEGV` to the task rather than
a machine halt. Fix the `rt_sigaction` bound to match the array.

### 4. There is no per-process resource accounting anywhere

Every object table in the kernel is global and fixed-size: 64 TCP connections, 256 rings, 32
AF_UNIX slots, 32 compositor client slots, 16 futex waiters per bucket, 16384 input-event
slots keyed by a never-recycled task id. One unprivileged process exhausts any of them and
denies the resource to every other process, permanently in most cases because there is no
reclaim path.

Concrete instances the audit confirmed: ~64 unacknowledged SYNs permanently wedge the TCP
stack (and the bounded SYN-queue defence in `net/src/tcp/listener.rs` is fully written,
fully tested, and **has no production caller** — `on_syn` is dead code); two socketpairs and
two `sendmsg` calls leak AF_UNIX slots with no cycle collector; one malformed length prefix
permanently wedges a compositor client slot.

**Fix.** This is the one finding that cannot be patched locally. It needs the process object
from #1 to hang counters off, then per-process caps enforced at each table's insert point.
Wire `tcp/listener.rs`'s existing SYN queue to the input path first — that defence is
already built and merely unreachable.

### 5. Two ELF gates fail open, and the kernel under test is exempt from both

`check_kernel_softfloat.sh` prints OK when its objdump cannot run, and
`check_stack_sizes.sh` prints OK on an ELF with no `.stack_sizes` section. Both currently
work on the real tree (44,219 stack-size records; the softfloat grep correctly flags 82 XMM
instructions when handed a vector-carrying ELF), so this is latent rather than active —
verification correctly downgraded it. But the *tests* kernel, the one that runs all 2716
tests, is exempt from both gates by design, and the release-profile kernel is never built or
tested anywhere in CI.

**Fix.** Fail closed on a missing tool or an empty section — the stack gate already does
this for `llvm-readobj` at `:43-49`; mirror that in the softfloat gate. Add a CI job that
builds the release kernel and runs both gates on it.

## What the documentation claims that the code does not support

This is the highest-leverage category in the audit, because a false claim in
`AGENTS.md`/`README.md`/`STATUS.md` is what stops the *next* bug from being found. None of
these is a vulnerability; all of them are load-bearing.

- **"Every line of `unsafe` in the entire kernel is confined to one trusted crate"**
  (`README.md:137-141`) is textually false in two ways. First, OSTD's exported macros expand
  to real `unsafe` blocks inside `forbid(unsafe_code)` crates — I counted **166 expansion
  sites** (105 × `write_field!`, 20 × `link_section_static!`, 20 × `hermetic_state!`, plus
  others), and neither `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]` (macro hygiene attributes the token to
  OSTD's lint context) nor `check_unsafe_outside_ostd.sh` (a source scanner; the call site
  has no `unsafe` keyword) can see them. Second, two vendored TCB annexes with 197 unsafe
  lines and 44,802 LoC link into `kernel.elf`, are exempt from the gate, and are absent from
  `STATUS.md`.

  **These expansions are sound** — `SlotPtr::from_raw` is an `unsafe fn`, so a `forbid`
  crate cannot fabricate one — and I verified that specifically before reporting it. The
  defect is that the *enforcement* is blind to a construct a future macro could drive
  through, and the claim overstates what is mechanised.

- **OSTD exports a safe raw-pointer vocabulary.** `util::ptr_buf` (whose own module doc
  concedes "the only thing that moves is the `unsafe` keyword's location"),
  `mm::hhdm_bytes`, `memory::memcpy`, `io_mem::register_io_mem_range` and
  `Frame::from_unused` are safe `pub fn`s taking raw addresses, with ~79-85 call sites in
  `forbid(unsafe_code)` crates. Verification found **no attacker-reachable path** — the
  syscall layer copies user buffers into kernel scratch before any of these sees a pointer
  — so this is API hygiene and TCB accounting, not a vulnerability. But it means
  `forbid(unsafe_code)` outside the TCB is a keyword property, not a memory-safety proof.

- **"Inv. 1–10"** is the vocabulary every SAFETY comment and the entire audited-only
  classification is written in. It is **defined nowhere in the repository**, and only 24 of
  651 SAFETY comments name one.

- **`AGENTS.md` says task-ownership invariants I1–I7 are machine-checked.** The proof checks
  a different set, T1–T7, and `STATUS.md` explicitly places four of the seven out of model.

- **The TCB ratio comparison is unit-inconsistent.** `STATUS.md` puts SlopOS's
  unsafe-line-*density* (0.53 %) in the same table column as Asterinas's and Theseus's
  crate/code-size TCB fractions. A like-for-like crate-granular recount is ~16 %. The
  verification pass noted the auditor's own 16 %/34 % counter-figures commit the same unit
  substitution, so the defensible statement is "the gap is far smaller than the table
  implies, possibly zero" — not a specific number. The method is disclosed one hop away in
  `scripts/tcb_ratio.sh:16-32`; `README.md:136-142` has no such pointer.

- **The "Linux-ABI" adjective** in the four-adjective public claim is false at the level the
  term means: syscall numbering is bespoke, so a Linux binary calling `read()` invokes yield
  and `stat()` spins the Wheel of Fate. ABI-*shaped* is true; ABI-*compatible* is not.

- **`STATUS.md` files three modules under "unaudited — pure safe Rust, no `unsafe`"** that
  contain 77 unsafe lines between them, including fn-pointer transmutes on the klog path and
  68 lines of raw CPU/GDT/page manipulation in the scaffolding all 2716 tests run under.

- **Four in-tree comments claim ring fds are close-on-fork.** No such mechanism exists.
  `ring/src/file_ops.rs:3-6` adds a fifth claiming exec teardown, which also does not exist.

- **`KNOWN_ISSUES.md`'s `pause_all_aps` entry describes a call path that no longer exists**,
  and the primitive is weaker than OSTD's docs claim for it.

## Three architectural decisions that will be regretted at 2× size

### Identity is resolved by linear scan of fixed static arrays, independently in every subsystem

`mm` has `PROCESS_VMS[256]`, `fs` has `PROCESS_TABLES[256]`, `sched` has the registry spine,
`ostd` has `PCR[256]`. Each is locally reasonable, which is why no single auditor flagged
it; the finding is the *sum*. There is no `struct process` that owns pointers to its parts,
so every subsystem re-derives identity by search, and the searches compose multiplicatively
on the page-fault path. Cost per operation is O(MAX_*), not O(live). This is also the root
cause of finding #1 above.

**Prior art.** Linux `task_struct` holds `mm`, `files`, `fs`, `signal` by pointer; pid
lookup happens once via the pid IDR, never on the fault path. Per-CPU areas are dynamically
allocated (Tejun Heo's percpu allocator, 2009) precisely to kill the `NR_CPUS`-sized static
tax. FreeBSD `struct proc` with hashed `pfind()`.

**Cost of delay.** One ~2k-line refactor across mm/fs/sched now; at 2× size it means every
subsystem that has since grown its own array, plus a `.bss` tax that by then may not fit in
early boot memory.

### Two incompatible teardown models coexist

`panic=unwind` runs destructors; cross-CPU kill abandons the stack without running them. So
every resource class needs a hand-written parking spine, and that count grows linearly with
the kernel. Invariant I8 ("no owning task handle may live in a stack frame that can
deschedule") is not a safety rule that happened to be needed — it is the *tax* the abandon
model levies, on every future blocking kernel path.

**The cheaper way to keep the recovery property.** Adopt Linux's rule: a task only ever
exits from its own context. Deliver kill as a flag; make every blocking primitive check it
and return `-ERESTARTSYS`/`-EINTR`, which frames unwind naturally by *returning*. The
ERESTARTSYS plumbing already exists for signals at `core/src/syscall/dispatch.rs:83-92`.
Then destructors always run, I8 evaporates, three parking spines get deleted, and
`panic=abort` becomes viable — reclaiming ~1.5 MB of `.eh_frame`, 58 kLoC from the TCB
annex, and the 2 KiB→4 KiB stack-frame ceiling that `panic=unwind` forced, which currently
weakens Inv. 5'.

### There is no deferred-work substrate

The model is one dedicated kernel thread per work source plus a global idle-loop callback
list. Neither scales in CPUs or in work sources. The tell: `sched/src/kthread.rs` is a full
72-line spawn/join API with **zero callers** — the generic mechanism was written, found
unusable, and bypassed. The ring auditor found the local symptom ("SlopRing has no
kernel-side async execution") and concluded it was a ring problem; it is not, SlopRing has
nowhere to defer *to*. `check_no_kernel_async.sh` forbids `async` in the kernel, which is a
defensible call, but nothing was built to fill the hole it leaves.

**Prior art.** Linux pre-2010 had exactly this model; Tejun Heo's concurrency-managed
workqueue (2.6.36) replaced it because thread count and per-thread stack cost blew up and
none could share CPU concurrency. Plus `irq_work` for the hardirq→process hop and per-CPU
`ksoftirqd`. FreeBSD `taskqueue(9)` with per-CPU `taskqgroup`.

**Cost of delay.** ~600 lines of new OSTD plus rewiring 5 call sites now; at 2× size,
15–20 singleton threads each with a 2 MiB kernel stack (which per another finding leak),
each an independent serialisation point, each with a bespoke waker protocol to re-audit.

## Scaling walls, in the order they will be hit

- **64 CPUs, hard correctness cliff.** The LUF deferred-TLB cross-CPU mask is a `u64` while
  `MAX_CPUS` is 256. Above 64 CPUs it silently drops shootdown targets.
- **RCU reclamation runs on CPU 0's idle loop only**, and the grace period is a sequential
  per-CPU busy-wait. Memory reclaim stops entirely whenever CPU 0 is busy, and gets slower
  with every CPU added.
- **One IRQ-disabling spinlock per process address space**, taken and dropped four times per
  page fault. No reader/writer split, no per-VMA lock — and no rmap, which is what would
  make one possible. Linux went rbtree → maple tree → per-VMA seqcount locks for exactly
  this path.
- **`TASK_MANAGER`'s global spinlock is taken twice per context switch**, inside the IRQ-off
  dispatch window.
- **Every blocking call takes all N CPUs' runqueue locks** and does N linear list scans, to
  remove a task that is provably in no queue.
- **MSI/MSI-X destination is hardcoded to APIC ID 0** while the IOAPIC path reads the real
  BSP id — every device interrupt lands on one CPU, and is misdelivered on any machine whose
  BSP LAPIC id is not 0.

## Absent mechanisms whose absence falsifies an existing guarantee

Filtered to absences with real consequences — "SlopOS has no IPv6" is not on this list.

- **No reverse mapping (rmap).** Rules out swap, page migration, compaction, memory hotplug,
  and safe truncate of a shared mapping — and means the kernel cannot answer "is this frame
  still mapped anywhere?", which several unmapping paths assume it can.
- **No fault-in path for kernel access to user memory.** No GUP, no CoW-break-on-write from
  kernel context, so a lazily-mapped or post-fork buffer makes every syscall that writes it
  return `EFAULT`.
- **No reclaim, no OOM policy, no watermarks, no reserve.** Memory exhaustion is
  unrecoverable by construction — which makes the fallible-allocation discipline
  (`KBox::try_*` everywhere) load-bearing with no counterpart that can ever *make* an
  allocation succeed. Worse, the unmap path needs a 2 KiB allocation to flush other CPUs'
  TLBs, so under pressure it silently skips the shootdown.
- **No interruptible-sleep discipline.** Interruptibility is opt-in per call site, and
  pipes, `waitpid` and futex opted out — while the trusted crate's own documentation names
  the pipe path as the exemplar of the discipline.
- **The reboot path never flushes the filesystem**, and the ext2 image carries no dirty-state
  word, so every reboot silently discards write-back data onto an image that still says it
  is clean.
- **No `exit_group` in slibc.** `exit()`, `abort()` and fatal signals terminate only the
  calling thread, leaving siblings running in a half-torn-down process.
- **No fuzzing, no property-based testing, no fault injection, no concurrency model
  checking.** 2,708 hand-written example tests and nothing else. Every finding in this audit
  is static reading; the *real* distribution of the concurrency bugs is unmeasured. For a
  kernel whose own history records a recurring lost-wake bug class, this is the largest
  single testing gap. The only liveness detector is a cross-CPU NMI buddy watchdog — no
  soft-lockup detector, no hung-task scanner, no per-test timeout, so a lost wake produces a
  silent 120 s stall with no artifact.

## Cross-subsystem seams

Bugs that live between two crates, where each side assumed the other checked.

- **`execve` is not an address-space boundary.** `do_exec` calls
  `process_vm_load_elf_data`, which unmaps exactly `[0x400000, 0x800000)` and nothing else.
  `teardown_inner_mappings` — which drains the VMA map and resets the heap — is never
  invoked on the exec path. The new program image inherits the old one's entire heap, mmap
  arena, shared-memfd mappings and SlopRing mappings. It does not show up in testing because
  the shell uses `spawn_path`, not `exec`.
- **`destroy_process_vm` can free the live PML4 of a thread still executing on another
  CPU.** `sched` decides liveness by task *status*; `mm`'s teardown safety depends on CR3
  *residency*; `task_terminate` deliberately sets the terminal status before the victim's
  CPU reaches a switch point. No file expresses the translation between the two definitions.
- **The page-fault handler runs on the non-reentrant IST4 stack with IF=0, then calls an
  allocator path that unconditionally executes `sti` and spins for cross-CPU acks.**
  `boot-arch` knew the first half; `mm` knew the second; the composition is stated nowhere.
- **virtio-net's RX ring shrinks monotonically.** One failed page allocation permanently
  retires an RX descriptor, and the only repost pass runs once at probe.
- **Any process can open any other session's pty slave by path.** `fs` turns `/dev/pts/N`
  into a tty slot index; the tty layer's only gate is the `SLAVE_LOCKED` bit that `grantpt`
  clears.

## Drift — one rule, several implementations

- **Five `align_up`s with five different overflow behaviours.** `abi/src/alignment.rs:20,46`
  saturates and is documented as canonical; `slopos-ostd/src/mm/heap.rs:137` — the kernel's
  *only* allocation surface — uses unguarded `(value + align - 1)`, which wraps to a small
  aligned value in release; `abi/src/ring.rs:737` is unguarded *inside the crate whose module
  doc claims canonical status*. Verified by hand.
- **Two UTF-8 decoders disagree on the same byte stream.** `vt/src/lib.rs:250-258` rejects
  overlong `C0`/`C1` and out-of-range `F5`-`F7` leads; `drivers/src/tty/ldisc.rs:220-235`
  accepts both. `C0 80` decodes to U+0000 through the ldisc and U+FFFD through the parser.
  `drivers/Cargo.toml:33` already depends on `slopos-vt`, so the strict decoder is one import
  away.
- **The whole syscall surface bypasses the `T: Pod` user-copy API.**
  `mm/src/user_copy.rs:81,87` forwards unconditionally to `copy_value_from_user<T: Copy>`,
  whose SAFETY comment asserts "the `T: Copy` contract permits arbitrary byte patterns to
  count as a valid `T`" — which is false; `Copy` implies no such thing, and `assume_init()`
  runs on attacker bytes. I traced the riskiest instantiation (`UserFsList`, which carries a
  `*mut UserFsEntry`) and it *is* re-validated through `MmUserBytes::try_new` before use, so
  this is a latent TCB soundness gap rather than a live bug. The `T: Pod` API has
  approximately zero production callers.
- **Six user-range validators with three different rules.** `mm/src/paging/tables.rs:151`
  `is_user_address` classifies the entire kernel direct map as a user address; its one caller
  ANDs it with `PageFlags::USER`, which contains the blast radius today.
- **Three filesystems, three rules for an over-long filename.** ext2 rejects with
  `ENAMETOOLONG`; ramfs silently truncates to 32 bytes at `fs/src/ramfs/mod.rs:84`, producing
  an entry its own `name_len == name.len()` lookup can never match — a permanent,
  unreclaimable inode leak reachable from userland.
- **A 468-line VT100 grid emulator implemented twice** (`drivers/src/tty/vconsole.rs` vs
  `terminal-core/src/grid.rs`), and a byte-identical 49-line syscall wrapper module
  (`slopos-rt/src/sys/ring.rs` vs `userland/src/syscall/ring.rs`).

## Build, CI and crate hygiene

- **No clippy anywhere.** `justfile:388` admits it; `justfile:436` points at a `clippy.toml`
  that does not exist; `[workspace.lints.clippy]` at `Cargo.toml:55-57` is never evaluated by
  any recipe or CI job.
- **Five kernel crates opt out of `[lints] workspace = true`** — `karch`, `hermetic`,
  `kernel-services`, `ktesting`, `service-core` — so `warnings = "deny"` does not apply to
  ~4,150 LoC, including the whole test harness.
- **`ktesting` is a normal, non-optional dependency of every kernel subsystem**, ships in
  every kernel image, contains 11 unconditional `unsafe` sites, is the only kernel-image
  crate missing `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]`, and three gate scripts exempt it as "userland".
  `scripts/kernel_crates.sh:9-11` documents the opposite of what it computes.
- **`karch` is a 60-line pure re-export shim** of `slopos-ostd` with 8 live consumers and two
  macros that silently discard their arguments.
- **15 stale manifest edges**, one of which (`drivers → slopos-core`, comment-only) is the
  sole reason the dependency graph appears to have an upward violation. Pruning the dead
  edges collapses depth 14→12 and puts `drivers` back below `core`. Otherwise: zero cycles,
  a strict DAG, and the actual layering matches the intended layering.
- **`sched/src/kthread.rs` is a fully unreferenced module**; `gfx`/`windowing`/`appkit`'s
  `alloc` feature is unreachable in every build configuration, making `HeadlessSurface`
  (~130 lines) uncompilable into anything; ~200 kernel-side `pub fn`s have zero call sites;
  6 test-only reset hooks are ungated into production.

---

*The appendix below lists every verified finding with its trigger, evidence, fix, prior art,
and any correction applied during adversarial verification.*

## Security severity: HIGH — 11 findings

### rt_sigreturn XRSTORs a fully attacker-controlled XSAVE header (no validation anywhere in the tree); the resulting kernel-mode #GP has no fixup and reaches the fatal panic path, halting the whole machine — an unprivileged local DoS, not memory corruption

`core crate` · confirmed as stated; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** Any unprivileged user process, two syscalls, deterministic: (1) `p = mmap(NULL, 8192, PROT_READ|PROT_WRITE, MAP_ANON|MAP_PRIVATE, -1, 0); memset(p, 0xFF, 8192);` (2) set RSP = p (or any address such that [p, p+160) and [p+160, p+160+2688) are mapped readable — `size_of::<SignalFrame>()` is 20*u64 = 160, abi/src/signal.rs:200-228 with `SigSet = u64` at :44; an earlier draft of this finding said 168, which is wrong) and execute `syscall(105)`. `read_signal_frame` succeeds, the GPR commit succeeds, `UserByt

**Evidence.**
- core/src/syscall/signal.rs:326-371 — `syscall_rt_sigreturn` reads the frame from the raw user `rsp` and then unconditionally calls `restore_fpu_from_sigframe(&current, rsp)` at :364-366; there is no cookie, magic, or 'a signal was actually delivered' check any
- core/src/syscall/signal.rs:306-324 — `restore_fpu_from_sigframe` does `copy_bytes_from_user(bytes, data)` into `&mut [u8; FPU_STATE_SIZE]` and then `current.task().fpu_restore_to_cpu(current, xcr0)`, with no inspection of the copied bytes
- slopos-ostd/src/task/kernel_task.rs:770-784 — `fpu_restore_to_cpu` goes straight to `fpu_xrstor(self.fpu_state.get_ptr(witness), xcr0_mask)`

**Fix.** Add a `validate_xstate_header(&[u8; FPU_STATE_SIZE]) -> Result<(), ()>` in `slopos-ostd` that (a) requires `xstate_bv & !active_xcr0() == 0`, (b) requires `xcomp_bv == 0` for the standard format (or the XCOMP_BV[63] compaction bit plus a subset check if XSAVEC is enabled), (c) requires reserved bytes 528..576 to be zero, and (d) masks MXCSR with `MXCSR_MASK` from the CPU. Call it in `restore_fpu_from_sigframe` before `fpu_restore_to_cpu` and retu

**Prior art.** Linux `arch/x86/kernel/fpu/xstate.c: validate_user_xstate_header()` is called from `copy_user_to_xstate()` on the sigreturn path and rejects any header whose xfeatures set bits outside `xfeatures_mask_user()`, whose reserved words are nonzero, or whose XCOMP_BV is inconsistent with the format; `os_xrstor_safe()` additi

**Corrected during verification.** Only the severity label. The mechanism, the missing validation, the absence of a fixup, and the fatal-panic routing are all exactly as described — I confirmed each independently, including the `interrupt_nesting_enter()` at boot/src/idt.rs:471 that the auditor did not cite but which is what actually forecloses the task-scoped panic-recovery escape hatch. ### PROCESS_TLB_INFO is a 256-entry array indexed by an unbounded monotonic PID, so targeted flushes for PID >= 256 silently no-op — but the scheduler refuses to dispatch any task with pid >= MAX_PROCESSES, so such an address space is never activated on any CPU and the no-op is semantically correct; the real defect is an availability cliff (process #256 since boot and every one after it is killed at f

`mm crate` · confirmed with corrections; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** For the claimed memory-corruption: none exists — no code path can activate an address space whose pid >= 256, because sched/src/scheduler.rs:1077-1084 terminates such a task before `prepare_switch_to` ever runs `process_vm_activate`. For the real (availability) defect: from any unprivileged shell, spawn 255 processes since boot (e.g. run 255 short-lived commands, or a `spawn`/`fork` loop — each `create_process_vm`/`process_vm_clone_cow` consumes 

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/memory_layout_defs.rs:370 — `pub const MAX_PROCESSES: usize = 256;`
- mm/src/tlb.rs:400-403 — `static PROCESS_TLB_INFO: [ProcessTlbInfo; MAX_PROCESSES]`
- mm/src/tlb.rs:406-412 — `fn process_tlb_info(process_id: u32) -> Option<&'static ProcessTlbInfo> { let idx = process_id as usize; if idx >= MAX_PROCESSES { return None; } ... }`

**Fix.** Index the shootdown state by the same key the address space itself uses — either the `PROCESS_VMS` slot index (bounded by MAX_PROCESSES by construction) or the `MmContextId` already threaded through `VmSpace::set_mm_ctx_handle`. Move `ProcessTlbInfo` into `ProcessVm` (or into `VmSpace`) so it cannot outlive or mis-alias its address space. As a stopgap, make `process_tlb_info` returning `None` a hard error rather than a silent `Ok(())`, and add a 

**Prior art.** Linux keys `mm_cpumask` off `struct mm_struct`, an object whose lifetime is the address space itself, never off a numeric PID; `flush_tlb_mm_range` takes `struct mm_struct *`. Any array indexed by a recycled or unbounded integer key is exactly the aliasing hazard `struct mm` exists to avoid. FreeBSD does the same via `

**Corrected during verification.** The auditor never checked whether a pid >= 256 address space can ever be *loaded* on a CPU. It cannot: `execute_task` (sched/src/scheduler.rs:1071-1085) refuses to dispatch any task with `pid >= MAX_PROCESSES` and terminates it, and that check runs before `prepare_switch_to`, which is the sole caller of `process_vm_activate` and `notify_mm_switch`. So for exactly the PIDs where `process_tlb_info` 

### `rt_sigaction` validates the signal number against 64 but indexes a 32-entry `signal_actions` array — any signum in 33..=64 with a non-null act/oldact pointer is an out-of-bounds index panic reachable from unprivileged userland

`core crate` · confirmed as stated

**Trigger.** Raw syscall from any unprivileged user task: `syscall(102 /* rt_sigaction */, sig, act, oldact, 8)` with `sig` anywhere in 33..=64 and at least one of the two pointers non-null and mapped. Concretely, the read path: rdi=33, rsi=0, rdx=<any valid user address with 32 writable bytes>, r10=8 → `MmUserPtr::try_new` succeeds, then `signal_actions[32]` on `[SignalActionCell; 32]` panics with "index out of bounds: the len is 32 but the index is 32" (cor

**Evidence.**
- core/src/syscall/args.rs:193-203 — `impl SyscallArg for Signum`: `if v == 0 || v > 64 { return Err(EINVAL) }`, doc at :182 says 'validated to fall inside 1..=64'
- abi/src/signal.rs:8 — `pub const NSIG: usize = 32;`
- slopos-ostd/src/task/kernel_task.rs:594 — `pub signal_actions: [SignalActionCell; NSIG]` (32 elements)

**Fix.** Change `Signum::from_raw` to bound against `slopos_abi::signal::NSIG` rather than the literal 64, and delete `parse_signum` in favour of the typed argument so there is exactly one validator. Better still, make the bound structural: have `Signum` carry the index and expose `fn index(self) -> usize` used by an accessor `Task::signal_action(Signum)` whose signature makes the array bound unreachable, and add a `const _: () = assert!(NSIG == <signal_a

**Prior art.** Linux `kernel/signal.c: do_sigaction()` opens with `if (!valid_signal(sig) || sig < 1 || (act && sig_kernel_only(sig))) return -EINVAL;` where `valid_signal()` is `sig <= _NSIG`, and `_NSIG` is the *same* constant that sizes `sighand->action[_NSIG]`. The array bound and the validator are derived from one constant by co

**Corrected during verification.** Nothing substantive. Two nuances: (a) the "aborts the whole CI suite" framing is hypothetical — no existing test issues rt_sigaction with signum > 32, so the tests ISO is not currently affected; and in the tests image the mechanism is that user_loop.rs skips installing the RecoveryGuard at all (the else branch), after which panic_handler_impl's `recovery_is_active()` check falls through to fatal —

### Server-side SYN_RECV PCBs occupy one of the kernel's 64 global connection slots and are never reclaimed (no SYN_RECV timeout, no SYN-ACK retransmit on that path), so ~64 unacknowledged SYNs to a listening port permanently exhaust the TCP table until reboot

`net crate` · confirmed with corrections; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** Precondition: the SlopOS host has at least one TCP socket in LISTEN (socket/bind/listen → net/src/tcp/mod.rs:371 `tcp::listen`), reachable by the attacker (QEMU SLIRP hostfwd or a real NIC). Attack: for each of the 16 shards, pick 4 source ports whose FNV-1a-over-(local_ip, local_port, remote_ip, remote_port) — computed offline, the hash is unkeyed (net/src/tcp/table.rs:142-156) — lands in that shard. Send 64 bare SYN segments (valid IPv4+TCP che

**Evidence.**
- net/src/tcp/pcb/listen.rs:98-105 — a SYN fills `actions.accepted` unconditionally; there is no backlog check, no queue, no drop policy
- net/src/tcp/mod.rs:194-197 — `install_accepted_child` calls `table::install_established(...)`, permanently consuming a shard slot, and discards the `Result` with `let _ =`
- net/src/tcp/table.rs:60-69 — `NUM_SHARDS = 16`, `SLOTS_PER_SHARD = 4`; total established-connection capacity for the whole kernel is 64

**Fix.** Add a `TimerKind::TcpSynAck` scheduled at install time for both SYN_SENT and SYN_RECV: retransmit the SYN/SYN-ACK with 1/2/4/8/16 s backoff and `table::release` the slot after the last attempt — the constants already exist as `SYN_RETRIES_MAX`/`SYN_ACK_BASE_DELAY_MS` in `net/src/tcp/listener.rs:48-56`. Better: route the listener path through the existing `TcpListenState` two-queue model (see the separate finding) so half-open state never occupies

**Prior art.** Linux allocates a lightweight `request_sock` on a bounded SYN queue (`tcp_max_syn_backlog`), retransmits the SYN-ACK with exponential backoff up to `tcp_synack_retries` and then reaps the entry, and falls back to SYN cookies (state encoded in the ISN, zero PCB allocated) when the queue overflows. FreeBSD uses a syncach

**Corrected during verification.** One overstatement, in the body rather than the headline: "The same is true of `SynSent` on the client side — a lost SYN is never retransmitted and the slot is never reclaimed." The no-retransmit half is correct, but the slot IS reclaimed on the blocking client path — `socket_connect` arms a 30 s deadline (net/src/socket.rs:1831) and on expiry calls `tcp::abort(tcp_idx)` (net/src/socket.rs:1871) → 

### `syscall_spawn_path` stores the user-supplied `SpawnAttrs.flags` word into `task.flags` unvalidated, letting any user task mint a child holding COMPOSITOR / DISPLAY_EXCLUSIVE / SYSTEM / NO_PREEMPT — the entirety of SlopOS's syscall privilege model (KERNEL_MODE is the one bit that is rejected, and SYSTEM does not affect panic-fatality for user tasks)

`core crate` · confirmed with corrections; found independently by 3 auditors; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** From any unprivileged user task, one syscall. In-tree userland form: `let attrs = SpawnAttrs { priority: 2 /*Normal*/, _pad: [0;3], flags: TASK_FLAG_COMPOSITOR /*0x10*/, _pad2: 0, actions_ptr: 0, actions_len: 0, sigdefault_mask: 0 };` then `syscall5(SYSCALL_SPAWN_PATH=64, path_ptr, path_len, 0, 0, &attrs as u64)` with `path = b"/bin/anything"` (the attacker's own binary works — it can branch on argv). The child comes up with `task.flags = 0x10|0x

**Evidence.**
- abi/src/spawn.rs:57-70 — `SpawnAttrs { priority: u8, _pad, flags: u16, _pad2, actions_ptr, actions_len, sigdefault_mask }`, doc comment 'Task flags (`TASK_FLAG_*`)'
- core/src/syscall/process_handlers.rs:178-179 — `let attrs = copy_from_user(attrs_user)` reads the whole struct from user memory
- core/src/syscall/process_handlers.rs:181-189 — the *priority* field is validated (`TaskPriority::try_from_u8`, then `KernelIo` rejected with a written rationale about starving user tasks); the flags field gets no equivalent treatment

**Fix.** Define `const SPAWN_USER_SETTABLE_FLAGS: u16 = TASK_FLAG_NEW_PGRP | TASK_FLAG_FOREGROUND;` in `abi/src/task.rs` and, in `syscall_spawn_path`, reject `attrs.flags & !SPAWN_USER_SETTABLE_FLAGS != 0` with EINVAL (reject, do not mask — masking makes the bits permanently unusable, per the extensible-ABI rule). The privileged flags must come from a caller-authority check: `TASK_FLAG_COMPOSITOR` should require the caller already be the compositor (or be

**Prior art.** Linux's `clone3`/`CLONE_*` flag word is validated bit-by-bit against `CLONE_LEGAL_FLAGS` in `copy_process()`, and every capability-bearing transition (`CAP_SYS_NICE` for priority, `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` for scheduling class) is checked against the *caller's* credentials at the point the authority is exercised, never inherited

**Corrected during verification.** Two things. (1) Escalation (2) is half wrong: setting TASK_FLAG_SYSTEM does NOT flip `fatal_if_panics` for a task spawned via `spawn_path`. Both sites that read TASK_FLAG_SYSTEM for panic-fatality (sched/src/task/task_lifecycle.rs:419 in `kernel_task_entry_shim`, sched/src/runtime.rs:84 in `kernel_thread_trampoline`) are on the kernel-thread entry path, and `init_task_context` (task_lifecycle.rs:4

### DataState::on_segment has no RFC 793 §3.9 acceptability test: any spoofed SYN on the 4-tuple tears down an established connection regardless of sequence number, ts_recent can be poisoned out-of-window, and data/FIN are accepted with an arbitrary ACK number

`net crate` · confirmed with corrections; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** Given an established connection between SlopOS 10.0.0.1:80 and peer 10.0.0.2:P (attacker knows or brute-forces the ~16-bit ephemeral port P): (1) Blind reset — ONE spoofed IPv4/TCP frame, src 10.0.0.2:P, dst 10.0.0.1:80, flags = SYN only, seq_num arbitrary (e.g. 0), correct TCP checksum. Path: ipv4.rs:211 dispatch_tcp -> tcp::input (mod.rs:100) -> table::find hits -> input_process_established -> Pcb::on_segment (pcb/mod.rs:115) -> DataState::on_s

**Evidence.**
- net/src/tcp/pcb/data.rs:318-320 — `if hdr.is_syn() { return Self::on_unexpected_syn(pcb, hdr, actions); }` runs before any sequence test; `_hdr` is unused inside the handler
- net/src/tcp/pcb/data.rs:422-431 — `on_unexpected_syn` unconditionally emits a RST, sets `actions.release = true`, and raises `RESET_RECEIVED`
- net/src/tcp/pcb/mod.rs:100-121 — `Pcb::on_segment` dispatches straight to the per-state handler; there is no in-window filter anywhere on the path

**Fix.** Add a single `fn segment_acceptable(seg_seq, seg_len, rcv_nxt, rcv_wnd) -> bool` implementing the RFC 793 §3.9 four-case table and call it at the top of `DataState::on_segment` (after the RST fast path, which has its own classifier). Route SYN-in-established through `challenge_ack::try_challenge_ack` + an ACK carrying `rcv_nxt` instead of `on_unexpected_syn`'s RST. Gate the `ts_recent` update and payload/FIN acceptance behind the same predicate, 

**Prior art.** RFC 793 §3.9 defines the four-case acceptability test; RFC 5961 §4 requires that a SYN in a synchronized state produce a *challenge ACK*, never a reset, and §5 requires that an unacceptable ACK produce a challenge ACK rather than acceptance. Linux implements this in `tcp_validate_incoming()` (which runs `tcp_sequence()

**Corrected during verification.** Nothing material in the code claims — every cited line says what the auditor says it says, and I could not find a guard. Three corrections, none of which change the defect: (1) Severity is overstated. This is a remote off-path connection DoS plus a reduction in blind-injection work factor; there is no memory corruption (the OOO path is bounded by RingBuffer::write_at_offset at slopos-ostd/src/ring

### The in-kernel DNS stub resolver accepts any UDP datagram whose source port is 53 — no source-IP, destination-IP or destination-port check — and its only anti-spoofing field is a transaction ID from a boot-constant sequential counter, so an attacker who can land a packet at the NIC during a ~3 s resolve window poisons that hostname's cache entry with an attacker-chosen address and TTL

`net crate` · confirmed with corrections; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** Boot the kernel with virtio-net on a reachable segment (tap/bridge, bare metal, or a UDP hostfwd). Userland calls `resolve("example.com")` (syscall 135) for a name not in the 16-entry cache. The kernel builds a query with ID 0x4242 (first lookup after boot) and sends it from UDP port 49152 + (0x4242 % 16384) = 49730 to the DHCP-supplied server, then waits up to 3000 ms. Concurrently, from ANY source address, the attacker sends (or floods at ~1 pk

**Evidence.**
- net/src/dns.rs:39 — `static QUERY_ID: AtomicU16 = AtomicU16::new(0x4242);` — a fixed boot constant
- net/src/dns.rs:662,686 — `QUERY_ID.fetch_add(1, Ordering::Relaxed)` is the only ID source; the first query after boot always has ID 0x4242
- net/src/dns.rs:796 — `let src_port = 49152 + (resolver.query_id() % 16384);` — the source port is a pure function of the transaction ID, contributing no independent entropy

**Fix.** Three independent fixes, all small: (1) seed `QUERY_ID` from the boot entropy source and draw each ID from a PRNG, not `fetch_add`; (2) allocate the source port independently of the ID from the ephemeral allocator; (3) move interception out of the driver into a proper UDP demux entry bound to `(dns_server_ip, 53, our_ip, src_port)` so the socket layer's existing 4-tuple match does the validation — `net/src/udp.rs`'s `UdpDemuxBucket::lookup_exact`

**Prior art.** RFC 5452 requires a randomised transaction ID *and* randomised source port (the 0x20 encoding is a further hardening), and requires that responses be matched on the full (src IP, src port, dst port, ID, question) tuple. Linux/glibc's resolver and every recursive resolver since 2008 do all of this; djbdns did it in 2001

**Corrected during verification.** Three overstatements, none fatal to the finding. (1) "One forged packet … inserts an attacker-chosen A record" with "no race at all" is not quite right: `dns_resolve` calls `dns_rx_clear()` before every transmit (net/src/dns.rs:798-801), so a datagram that arrives while no query is outstanding is discarded. The packet must land inside the ≤3000 ms `dns_rx_wait` window (DNS_TIMEOUT_MS, 3 attempts).

`net crate` · confirmed with corrections; found independently by 2 auditors; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** 1. Attacker sends a SYN to any open TCP port on the SlopOS host with the RFC 7323 timestamp option set (or induces the host to `connect()` out, which unconditionally sends `with_timestamp(now_ms as u32, 0)` at net/src/tcp/mod.rs:366). 2. From the returned SYN-ACK, read ISS (the sequence number, = `generate_isn(tuple)`) and TSval (= `uptime_ms as u32`) — both are in the same segment. 3. For each d in [TSval*250, TSval*250+250): set x = (ISS - d) m

**Evidence.**
- net/src/tcp/isn.rs:61-68 — `FNV_PRIME = 0x0000_0100_0000_01B3`; `fnv_mix(h,b) = (h ^ b).wrapping_mul(FNV_PRIME)`
- net/src/tcp/isn.rs:72-98 — `generate_isn` seeds `h = FNV_OFFSET ^ boot_secret()`, mixes 12 known 4-tuple bytes, and returns `(h as u32).wrapping_add(drift)` where `drift = (monotonic_ns() / 4_000) as u32`
- net/src/tcp/isn.rs:21-25 — the module doc: "This is intentionally **not** a keyed hash … The design is strictly better than the predictable counter it replaces", and :4-5 claims the result is "unpredictable to off-path attackers"

**Fix.** Replace the FNV mix with a real PRF. SipHash-2-4 is ~60 lines of pure integer Rust with no dependency and belongs in `abi` or `slopos-ostd` where the neighbor/handle hashes can share it. Keep the RFC 6528 clock term but derive it from a source that is not simultaneously published in the timestamp option (e.g. add a second secret-derived offset), and seed the boot secret from a proper entropy source rather than `monotonic_ns()` at first call. Also

**Prior art.** RFC 6528 requires ISN = M + F(localip, localport, remoteip, remoteport, secretkey) with F a *cryptographic* hash. Linux's `secure_tcp_seq()` used MD5 and now uses SipHash over the 4-tuple plus a per-boot `net_secret`; the key is never derivable from any observable output because the hash is one-way. **Corrected during verification.** Two overstatements, neither of which rescues the code. (1) The title claims one observed connection recovers "the boot secret". Only `secret & 0xFFFF_FFFF` is ever recoverable — the high 32 bits never influence the output — and one observation leaves 250 candidates because of the unknown sub-millisecond part of `drift`; a second connection on a different 4-tuple is required to pin it (the finding'

### On any CPU where CR4.PCIDE is enabled, OSTD assigns PCIDs from a global 12-bit-masked monotonic counter with no reuse/flush tracking and writes every CR3 with NOFLUSH, so address-space creation #4096 takes Pcid::KERNEL and #4096+k aliases address space #k's TLB tag; nothing ever flushes a retired PCID, and the correct per-CPU ASID pool (mm::mmu::asid::select_cr3) has no callers

`OSTD memory core` · confirmed as stated; found independently by 2 auditors

**Trigger.** On any CPU where CR4.PCIDE gets enabled at boot (CPUID.1:ECX.PCID set and not an Alder/Raptor Lake model per mm/src/mmu/errata.rs), have unprivileged userland spawn/fork 4096 times — each `create_process_vm`/`process_vm_clone_cow` (sched/src/task/task_lifecycle.rs:91,110) calls `VmSpace::new` and bumps NEXT_PCID. - slopos-ostd/src/mm/vm_space.rs:287-298 — comment "PCID assignment. Monotonic counter, **never reused**, masked to the architectural 12 bits." then `fn alloc_pcid() -> Pcid { let raw = NEXT_PCID.fetch_add(1, Relaxed); Pcid::new((raw & 0x0FFF) as u16) }`. The ma
- slopos-ostd/src/arch/x86_64/cr3.rs:20-28 — `Pcid::KERNEL = Pcid(0)`; `NEXT_PCID` starts at 1, so `4096 & 0x0FFF == 0` hands a user address space the kernel's PCID
- slopos-ostd/src/mm/vm_space.rs:336 — `pcid: alloc_pcid()` in `VmSpace::new`, i.e. one PCID consumed per process creation, never released

Wire `forget_context_local` into `VmSpace::drop`. If that is too large a step, t

**Prior art.** x86 Linux keeps a 6-entry per-CPU `cpu_tlbstate.ctxs[]` array mapping ASID slot → (mm, `tlb_gen`), assigns the hardware PCID as `slot + 1`, and on every switch compares the mm's current `tlb_gen` against the slot's; equal ⇒ CR3 with NOFLUSH, stale ⇒ INVPCID single-context then NOFLUSH, miss ⇒ rotate, flush the evicted

### Multi-page buddy allocations (count > 1) skip the LUF reuse drain; the exploitable window is the un-drained brk-shrink / process_vm_free unmap paths, NOT munmap (which drains) and NOT the count==1 path (whose needle is exact)

`mm crate` · confirmed with corrections; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** SMP required. Process A has two threads sharing one VmSpace. T1 is resident on CPU1 and reads/writes heap page P, caching a writable translation in CPU1's TLB. T0 on CPU0 calls brk(new_brk) shrinking the heap past P: process_vm_brk -> unmap_and_free_range_inner -> ostd_unmap_4kb_user -> LufHook::after_unmap -> luf::queue_unmap, which only INVLPGs CPU0 and parks (vaddr, P) in CPU0's ring; the returned UFrame drops and P is freed (buddy.rs:812), go

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/page_alloc/buddy.rs:1058-1068 — `// LUF reuse-drain hook: ... Single-page allocations only, matching the pre-refactor behaviour (multi-page callers do their own TLB management). if !phys.is_null() && count == 1 { if !crate::mmu::luf::drain_if_reusing_fr
- mm/src/page_alloc/mod.rs:92-94 — `__alloc_page_frames_raw(count, flags)` calls `alloc_raw` with no drain at all
- mm/src/memfd.rs:212 — `let phys = alloc_kernel_pages(page_count);` where `page_count = aligned_size / 4096` comes straight from a userland `ftruncate` on a memfd

**Prior art.** Linux never lets a page leave the mmu_gather batch before `tlb_finish_mmu`, so the question of the allocator re-checking never arises; the invariant is enforced at free time, not at alloc time. Any scheme that checks at alloc time must key the check on the whole allocated *range*, not on a single PFN — this is the same

**Evidence.**
- net/src/unix_socket_file_ops.rs:26-30 — `impl Drop for UnixSocketBacking { fn drop(&mut self) { let _ = unix_socket::unix_close(self.handle); } }` — the endpoint is closed only when the last `FileRef` drops
- net/src/unix_socket/mod.rs:929 — `unix_close` is what calls `slots.remove(handle.handle())`; the slot is not freed by the `close(2)` syscall itself
- net/src/unix_socket/pair.rs:46-65 — `AncillaryQueue` holds owning `FileRef`s until the receiver drains them

**Fix.** Forbid the cycle structurally rather than collect it. The cheapest sound rule: refuse (`-EOPNOTSUPP`) to pass any fd whose `FileOps::is_unix_socket()` is true — the predicate already exists and the compositor/clipboard use cases pass memfds, not sockets. If socket passing must be supported, cap the passing depth by stamping a `scm_depth: u8` on each endpoint (depth = 1 + max depth of anything in its queues) and rejecting a send that would exceed 

**Prior art.** Linux needed a dedicated in-kernel garbage collector (`net/unix/garbage.c`) with a decade of CVEs (CVE-2021-0920, CVE-2024-26923, plus UAFs introduced by the 6.10 Tarjan-SCC rewrite) precisely because it allowed the cycle. The brief's own antipattern list is explicit that a small kernel should structurally forbid the c

**Corrected during verification.** Four errors. (1) In the two-pair cycle it describes, only 2 of the 4 slots leak — s2 and s4 are freed normally because nothing holds an alias to their open files, so each pair merely drops 2→1; "all four slots leak permanently" is false. (2) "Eight socketpairs and eight sendmsg calls consume the entire kernel AF_UNIX slot table" is off by 2x-4x: 8 cycles leak 8 of 32 slots. (3) The wrong resource 


## Security severity: MEDIUM — 36 findings

### ramfs unlink frees the inode slot immediately and alloc_inode hands the same id to the next create, so an fd left open across unlink+create silently reads/writes a different file (integrity bug; no privilege boundary is crossed because SlopOS enforces no file permissions at all)

`fs crate` · confirmed with corrections; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** From an ordinary unprivileged userland process, against the ramfs mounted at /tmp (fs/src/vfs/init.rs:26), with /tmp empty so slot 2 is the lowest free id: 1. fd = open("/tmp/a", O_RDWR|O_CREAT) [SYSCALL_FS_OPEN=14] -> vfs create -> alloc_inode returns id 2; OpenVnode{fs=RAMFS_TMP_STATIC, inode=2} is stored. 2. unlink("/tmp/a") [SYSCALL_FS_UNLINK=20] -> ramfs/mod.rs:399 resets slot 2 (in_use=false, data freed). fd is still open and still names in

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/ramfs/mod.rs:399 — `unlink` ends with `inner.inodes[target_id as usize].reset();`, and `reset` (:56-66) sets `in_use = false` and frees the data
- fs/src/ramfs/mod.rs:153-159 — `alloc_inode` scans `(ROOT_INODE+1)..len` and returns the *first* `!in_use` slot, i.e. the id just freed
- fs/src/vfs_file_ops.rs:21-24 — `struct OpenVnode { fs: &'static dyn FileSystem, inode: InodeId }` — an open fd stores a bare inode number with no generation

**Fix.** Two-stage fix. Short term: make `InodeId` a `(number, generation)` pair (or bump a per-slot generation in `RamInode::reset`) and have every `FileSystem` method validate the generation, returning `ESTALE` on mismatch — the `HandleTable` in `slopos-ostd` already implements exactly this pattern and `vfs_file_ops.rs` already uses it one level up. Long term: introduce a refcounted `Inode` object owned by the VFS (`KArc<Inode>`) that `OpenVnode` holds,

**Prior art.** Linux and FreeBSD both interpose a refcounted object whose existence pins the identity — `struct inode` under `i_count`/`i_nlink` (unlink only decrements `i_nlink`; `iput()` frees when both hit zero) and FreeBSD's `v_usecount`/`v_holdcnt` split. NFS-style file handles pair the inode number with a generation counter for

**Corrected during verification.** Two overstatements. (1) Severity/impact: the report frames this as a permission-crossing disclosure ("a file the holder of fd may have no permission relationship with") and rates it critical. SlopOS performs no file access-control checks anywhere in the open path (fs/src/vfs/ops.rs:71-99 and fs/src/fileio/fdops.rs:105-191 check no mode/uid), ramfs reports uid=gid=0 for every inode, and syscall_get

### Mount resolution is a textual longest-prefix match on the raw, unnormalised user path, so `//tmp/x` and `/./tmp/x` (and `/dir/../tmp/x`) fall through to the root filesystem and reach the directory the mount shadows; `..` cannot cross a mount boundary at all (it fails closed inside the already-selected fs). No privilege boundary is crossed — SlopOS has no uid model, no VFS permission checks, and no

`fs crate` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** From any unprivileged userland process (no shell — the shell normalises): 1) `mkdir("/tmp")` — vfs_mkdir resolves the parent to the ROOT filesystem's root inode and creates a real `tmp` directory underneath the ramfs mount (returns 0, or EEXIST if the initramfs already made one, in which case the shadow already exists). 2) `fd = open("//tmp/secret", O_CREAT|O_RDWR)` — resolve_mount("//tmp/secret") compares `&path[..4] == b"//tm"` against `b"/tmp"

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/vfs/mount.rs:102-111 — `let matches = if mp_path == b"/" { true } else if path.len() >= mp_path.len() { &path[..mp_path.len()] == mp_path && (path.len() == mp_path.len() || path.get(mp_path.len()) == Some(&b'/')) }` — a raw byte-prefix compare against t
- fs/src/vfs/mount.rs:212-225 — `resolve_mount` splits the path once, textually, and hands the remainder to the matched filesystem
- fs/src/vfs/path.rs:14-32 — `resolve_path` calls `resolve_mount(path)` exactly once, then walks components entirely inside that one filesystem; a mount point crossed mid-walk is never detected

**Fix.** Move the mount check into the per-component walk: after resolving each component to an inode, consult the mount table for a mount rooted at that `(fs, inode)` and switch filesystems if one exists; keep a stack of `(fs, inode)` crossings so `..` at a mount root pops back to the parent mount's mountpoint. **Prior art.** Linux does not resolve mounts textually at all: `link_path_walk` steps component by component and `__follow_mount_rcu`/`follow_managed` checks `d_mountpoint()` at *each* step, with `..` handled by `follow_dotdot` which explicitly crosses back out of a mount via `mnt_parent`/`mnt_mountpoint` and validates against `mount

**Corrected during verification.** Two things. (1) The `..` half of the title is false: once resolve_mount picks a filesystem, `..` is resolved by that filesystem (path.rs:24) and can never cross a mount boundary — `/tmp/../etc/x` stays inside the /tmp ramfs and returns ENOENT, `/dev/../tmp/x` stays inside devfs. `..` fails closed; it only reaches shadowed content in paths that already failed the textual prefix match (e.g. `/a/../t

### Directory listings are hard-capped at 64 entries (~62 visible after `.`/`..`) by three independent limits — the ABI constant USER_FS_MAX_ENTRIES, the syscall's EINVAL on larger buffers, and vfs_list's [0u64; 64] scratch array — and the fs_list ABI has no cursor, so larger directories are silently truncated with an Ok return

`fs crate` · confirmed as stated

**Trigger.** From userland on the ramfs root: create a directory and populate it with 63 or more files, e.g. `mkdir("/many")` then `open("/many/f000", O_CREAT|O_WRONLY, ...)` ... `open("/many/f062", ...)` (63 files; plus the auto-created `.` and `..` entries that ramfs/mod.rs:355-356 adds, that is 65 dirents). Then call `fs_list("/many", &UserFsList{entries, max_entries: 64, count: 0})` — equivalently `std::fs::read_dir("/many")` or the shell's `ls /many`. Th

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/vfs/ops.rs:153 — `let mut inodes = [0u64; 64];` — a fixed scratch array used to re-stat children after the readdir callback
- fs/src/vfs/ops.rs:158 — `if count >= max || count >= 64 { return false; }` — the callback stops unconditionally at 64 regardless of the caller's buffer size
- fs/src/vfs/ops.rs:143 — `pub fn vfs_list(path: &[u8], entries: &mut [UserFsEntry]) -> VfsResult<usize>` — no offset/cursor parameter

**Fix.** Change the ABI to a cursor: put a `u64 cookie` in `UserFsList` (in and out), thread it through `file_list_path` → `vfs_list` → `FileSystem::readdir`'s existing `offset`, and drop the `[0u64; 64]` scratch array in favour of stat-ing each child inside the callback (or extend `readdir`'s callback signature to carry the size). Ideally key the cursor off the open file description like `getdents` does rather than off a path. In the interim, at minimum 

**Prior art.** `getdents64(fd, buf, count)` is stateful in the *open file description* — the kernel stores `f_pos` and each call resumes from it, so arbitrarily large directories stream in bounded buffers. ext4 additionally uses an htree cookie rather than a linear entry index so resumption is O(log n) and stable across concurrent mo

**Corrected during verification.** Two refinements, neither of which undermines the finding. (1) The cap is attributed solely to the `[0u64; 64]` scratch array, but there are three independent 64-limits: the ABI constant `USER_FS_MAX_ENTRIES` (abi/src/fs.rs:7), the syscall's `cap > USER_FS_MAX_ENTRIES -> EINVAL` rejection (path_handlers.rs:111), and the scratch array. Deleting the scratch array as the recommendation suggests would

### ramfs `rename` performs no ancestor, self-, or target-type checks and never `reset()`s the displaced target inode: a userland `rename(2)` can splice a directory into its own descendant (permanently unreachable, unremovable cycle) and every overwrite-rename leaks the victim inode and its data until RAMFS_MAX_INODES = 4096 is exhausted — availability-only, no memory unsafety

**Trigger.** Directly from an unprivileged userland process, no special setup. (1) Self-descendant cycle: `mkdir("/tmp/a"); mkdir("/tmp/a/b"); rename("/tmp/a", "/tmp/a/b/c");` — resolve_parent("/tmp/a/b/c") succeeds (parent `b` exists), both operands are on RAMFS_TMP_STATIC so the CrossDevice check passes, `a` is unlinked from the tmpfs root and installed as `c` inside its own child `b`, and `a`'s `..`/`parent` are rewritten to `b`. `/tmp/a` now returns NotFo

- fs/src/ramfs/mod.rs:479-483 — `if new_parent_node.lookup(new_name).is_ok() { inner.get_inode_mut(new_parent)?.remove_dir_entry(new_name)?; }` — the displaced entry is unlinked from the directory but its `RamInode` is never `reset()`

**Corrected during verification.** Only the severity framing. The mechanism is reported accurately in all three parts (missing ancestor check, missing reset() of the displaced inode, missing POSIX target-type checks). (1) Immediately: add `requires(console_admin)` to `syscall_halt` and `syscall_reboot`, and add a caller-vs-target check to `syscall_kill` (same session, or caller is the target's parent, or caller is console_admin) and to `syscall_set_cpu_affinity`/`syscall_waitpid`. ### `mprotect` issues no cross-CPU TLB shootdown — the mechanism is exactly as described, but the blast radius is confined to the caller's own address space (no cross-process or kernel-memory exposure), so this is a POSIX-correctness / in-process-hardening defect, not a privilege boundary break

### No per-process resource accounting exists: every kernel object table (pipes, memfds, unix pairs, sockets, rings, vnodes) is a single global fixed-capacity table with no charge/uncharge per process, so one process can exhaust a table system-wide; the per-process fd table is a hard 32-entry array

**Trigger.** Single unprivileged process, no fork needed: `for i in 0..64 { let fd = memfd_create(0); ftruncate(fd, 4096); mmap(NULL, 4096, PROT_READ|PROT_WRITE, MAP_SHARED, fd, 0); close(fd); }`. Each iteration consumes one of the 64 fixed registry slots; close(fd) drops refcount to 0 but map_count is 1, so try_cleanup (mm/src/memfd.rs:115) declines to free, and the process holds at most one fd at a time — the 32-fd per-process cap never bites. ### ext2 `create_inode_entry` performs no duplicate-name check, so a second `mkdir` of an existing name succeeds and writes a second directory record with a second inode (the O_CREAT file path is separately guarded by a VFS pre-lookup, and the initramfs-unpacker consequence is not reachable in any current boot configuration)

**Evidence.**

**Prior art.** Linux's VFS makes this structurally impossible: `filename_create()` does a `lookup_hash()` and returns `-EEXIST` before `vfs_mkdir()` is ever called, with the parent's `i_rwsem` held exclusive across both so the check and the create are atomic. ext4's `ext4_add_entry` additionally re-checks and returns `-EEXIST`.

### Futex buckets cap waiters at 16 per bucket (address-agnostic slots, 64 buckets, global across processes) and return -ENOMEM, which every slibc/std futex wrapper discards — so the 17th concurrent waiter in a bucket busy-spins in a syscall loop instead of blocking; needs 18 mutex threads (not 17), and the mutex case still makes progress

`sched crate` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Local userland process, production kernel. Spawn 18 threads all contending one pthread_mutex_t (or 17 threads all in pthread_cond_wait on one condvar) whose futex word hashes — via `(addr >> 2).wrapping_mul(0x9E3779B97F4A7C15) & 63` — into a bucket with no other waiters. The 17th thread to block enters syscall_futex/FUTEX_WAIT, finds no free slot at futex.rs:146-151, and gets -12 back immediately; slibc's `let _ = Sys::futex_wait(...)` drops it a

**Evidence.**
- sched/src/futex.rs:20-23 — `const FUTEX_HASH_BUCKETS: usize = 64;` and `const FUTEX_MAX_WAITERS_PER_BUCKET: usize = 16;`
- sched/src/futex.rs:55-58 — `struct FutexBucket { waiters: [FutexWaiter; FUTEX_MAX_WAITERS_PER_BUCKET], count: usize }` — a fixed array, not a list
- sched/src/futex.rs:144-154 — the free-slot search; `let Some(idx) = slot_idx else { return slopos_abi::syscall::ERRNO_ENOMEM as i64; }`

**Fix.** Replace the fixed array with the intrusive list primitive the crate already uses for ready queues: give `Task` a `FutexWaitRole` link, park the bucket's owning `TaskRef` via `task_placement_retain` exactly as `ReadyQueue` does, and enqueue with `IntrusiveLinkedList::push`. That removes the cap, removes the O(16) linear scans in `futex_wake` and `futex_remove_task`, and allocates nothing. Until then, at minimum make slibc's `pthread_mutex_lock` tr

**Prior art.** Linux's futex hash buckets are `struct futex_hash_bucket { spinlock_t lock; struct plist_head chain; }` — an unbounded priority-ordered list; the waiter's `struct futex_q` lives on the *waiter's own kernel stack*, so enqueue allocates nothing and cannot fail. Linux 6.16 further moved to per-mm private hash tables to re

**Corrected during verification.** Four factual errors, none fatal to the core claim. (1) Off-by-one repro: 17 threads on one pthread_mutex_t produce at most 16 blocked waiters, which fit the 16 slots exactly; you need 18 threads on a mutex (or 17 on a condvar) to hit ENOMEM. (2) "under a 100 ms quantum" — the quantum is SCHED_DEFAULT_TIME_SLICE = 10 (sched/src/scheduler.rs:152). (3) "issues a syscall per iteration at 100% CPU fore

`net crate` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Off-path attacker A on the same network as SlopOS host V, with the ability to spoof source IPs and to hold its own TCP connection to V. (1) A opens a normal connection C_A to a listening port on V and reaches ESTABLISHED (Data PCB). (2) A picks a target 4-tuple (V:port ↔ peer:port) it wants to attack. (3) In one 1 s epoch: A sends k spoofed RST segments with the victim tuple and seg_seq = candidate blocks spaced by the window size (each hits data

**Evidence.**

- net/src/tcp/challenge_ack.rs:96-110 — `try_challenge_ack(now_ms)` increments the single global counter and returns `prev < CHALLENGE_ACK_LIMIT`
- net/src/tcp/challenge_ack.rs:26-29 — the module doc: "enforces a global per-epoch cap on challenge ACKs (default 1 000 per second, matching Linux's `tcp_challenge_ack_limit`)"

**Fix.** Move the counter into `DataState` (a `u8` epoch counter plus a `u32` epoch start is 12 bytes on a struct that already carries 27 fields), and jitter the per-socket limit with a per-connection random offset so the exact threshold is not observable. Delete the global statics entirely rather than keeping them as a second-tier cap — a global fallback reintroduces the same channel. Update the module doc to cite post-4.7 Linux, since the current text p

**Prior art.** Linux commit 75ff39c ('tcp: make challenge acks less predictable') moved the budget per-socket via `sk->sk_rcv_challenge` semantics and randomised the limit with `prandom_u32_max()`. FreeBSD has always rate-limited per-connection via `V_tcp_ack_war_*` counters on the tcpcb.

**Corrected during verification.** Three things. (1) "CVE-2016-5696 verbatim" and the stated leak "whether the guessed 4-tuple exists" are wrong: SlopOS emits a challenge ACK only for an in-window-inexact RST on an ESTABLISHED Data PCB. A spoofed SYN (the paper's 4-tuple oracle) consumes no token — data.rs:422 answers it with an unconditional bare RST — and an unacceptable ACK number (the paper's stage-3 oracle) produces no segment

### O_APPEND is a one-shot seek-to-end at open and the shared file offset has no lock, so two *concurrently open* descriptions (or one description shared by two runnable tasks) clobber/skip data; the ordinary single-description sequential-append case is correct

`fs crate` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Deterministic, no race required (single process, single CPU): `fd1 = open("/log", O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_APPEND)`; `fd2 = open("/log", O_WRONLY|O_APPEND)` — both `install_fd_entry` calls snapshot `ops.size()` = N; `write(fd1, "aaa", 3)` puts bytes at N and advances only fd1's `OpenFile.position`; `write(fd2, "bbb", 3)` still uses its stale N and overwrites "aaa". From the shell: `progA >> /log & progB >> /log &` (userland/src/apps/shell/exec.rs:561, 

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/fileio/fdops.rs:42-48 — `if flags.contains(OpenMode::APPEND) { match ops.size(handle) { Some(size) => position = size, ... } }` — the append offset is captured *once*, at open
- fs/src/fileio/fdops.rs:295 — `let used_offset = if seekable { open_file.position() };` — the write path reads the stored position, never re-queries the size, and ignores the APPEND bit
- fs/src/vfs_file_ops.rs:142 — the ext2/ramfs `FileOps::write` signature takes `_flags: u32` and discards it, so the append bit never reaches a filesystem either

**Fix.** Add a `pos_lock` (a `PreemptMutex` or a per-description `SpinLock`) to `OpenFile`, acquired by `read_open_file`/`write_open_file`/`file_seek_fd` only when `KArc::strong_count > 1`, covering the load-I/O-store as one unit. Separately, stop snapshotting the size at open: keep `OpenMode::APPEND` in the status flags (it already is) and have `write_open_file` recompute `used_offset = ops.size(handle)` when the bit is set, ideally inside the filesystem

**Prior art.** Linux serialises the shared offset with `f_pos_lock` in `fdget_pos()`/`fdput_pos()` (taken only when `f_count > 1`, so single-owner fds pay nothing), and implements `O_APPEND` inside the write path: `generic_write_checks()` sets `iocb->ki_pos = i_size_read(inode)` while holding the inode's `i_rwsem` exclusive, so appen

**Corrected during verification.** Two calibration errors, no factual ones. (1) Severity: rated high, but the common append pattern is correct — each `open` re-snapshots the size and `fetch_add` advances the offset after every write, so sequential `>>` and any single-writer description behave properly; damage requires two concurrently-open descriptions or one description raced by two runnable tasks. **Evidence.**

### slibc's FILE has one buf/buf_pos/buf_len triple with no direction state, so any direction switch on an r+/w+/a+ stream loses buffered writes or flushes read-ahead bytes into the file — real, and worse than claimed (fseek/fflush/fclose also flush read-ahead), but latent: `fopen` has zero callers anywhere in the tree, so no shipped program can reach it

`userland platform` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** No trigger exists in the current tree (nothing calls fopen). For a future app linking slibc, the minimal trigger is: `FILE *f = fopen(p, "r+"); char b[100]; fread(b, 1, 100, f); fwrite(x, 1, 5000, f); fclose(f);` — fill_read_buf leaves buf[0..4096] = file bytes and buf_pos = 100; fwrite fills buf[100..4096] with caller data and flushes buf[0..4096], so 100 bytes of the file's own content are written at the fd's offset 4096, ahead of the caller's 

**Evidence.**
- slibc/src/stdio/mod.rs:74-89 — `FILE { fd, buf: [u8; BUFSIZ], buf_pos, buf_len, flags, mode, ungot }`. `buf_pos` is documented as 'Current position in the buffer (read or write cursor)' — one field for both directions, and there is no last-operation-direction 
- slibc/src/stdio/mod.rs:145-165 — `fill_read_buf` unconditionally does `self.buf_pos = 0; self.buf_len = 0;` and then `read()`s into `self.buf`, with no check for pending buffered writes.
- slibc/src/stdio/file.rs:136-140 — `fread` calls `fill_read_buf()` whenever `buf_pos >= buf_len`, which is true immediately after a buffered write (`buf_len` is 0 in write mode). The k bytes of buffered writes are overwritten and lost.

**Fix.** Either split the cursors (musl model: separate read and write ranges over the same backing array) or, minimally, add a `dir: Read|Write|None` field to `FILE` and make `fill_read_buf` flush a pending write buffer first and `fwrite` discard/seek-back a pending read buffer first. Add stdio tests covering fread→fwrite and fwrite→fread on an `r+` stream that assert the file content byte-for-byte.

**Prior art.** musl's FILE carries independent `rpos`/`rend` and `wpos`/`wend`/`wbase` pointers, and `__towrite`/`__toread` perform the direction switch (flushing or discarding correctly) before any transfer. glibc's `_IO_FILE` has `_IO_read_base/_IO_read_ptr/_IO_read_end` and `_IO_write_base/_IO_write_ptr/_IO_write_end` for the same

**Corrected during verification.** Two things. (1) Severity: the auditor rates this high, but `fopen` (and `fread`/`fwrite`/`setvbuf`) have zero callers in the entire repository — the only textual occurrence of `fopen` in any Rust source is its own definition at slibc/src/stdio/file.rs:42, and there are no C sources outside third_party/. ### ext2 unlink reclaims only direct + single-indirect blocks; every block reachable through i_block[13] (double) or i_block[14] (triple) is permanently leaked on the deleted file's mount

`fs crate` · confirmed as stated

**Trigger.** On any ext2-backed mount (`/` on the virtio-disk boot path, `/mnt` when an initramfs RAM root is active), with block_size 4096 / ptrs_per_block 1024: (1) `open("/big", O_CREAT|O_WRONLY)`; (2) `write()` until the file exceeds 4,243,456 bytes — file block 1036 is the first that routes through `i_block[13]`, causing `ensure_data_block` to allocate a double-indirect block, a level-2 indirect block, and the data block; (3) `close()`; (4) `unlink("/big

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/ext2/mod.rs:517-573 — `release_file_blocks` frees `inode.block[0..12]` (`.take(12)`, :518) and then handles `inode.block[12]` (:529-571). `inode.block[13]` and `inode.block[14]` are never touched.
- fs/src/ext2/blockmap.rs:41-56 — `block_to_path` fully supports depth 3 (`DINDIRECT_IDX`) and depth 4 (`TINDIRECT_IDX`)
- fs/src/ext2/blockmap.rs:127-166 — `ensure_data_block` allocates the intermediate indirect blocks for any depth, so `write_file` really does populate `block[13]`/`block[14]`

**Fix.** Make `unlink_entry` call `file::truncate(&mut inode, 0, ...)` — which already implements the full three-level free — instead of the partial `release_file_blocks`, and delete `release_file_blocks`. That closes this leak and simultaneously gives ext2 a working truncate (see the unwired-functionality finding). Before doing so, fix `free_indirect`'s 4 KiB stack array (fs/src/ext2/file.rs:183), which currently only escapes the 2 KiB stack gate because

**Prior art.** ext2 in Linux does this in `ext2_truncate_blocks` → `ext2_free_branches()`, which recurses over all three indirect levels; `ext2_evict_inode` calls truncate-to-zero before releasing the inode precisely so the reclaim path is shared and cannot diverge between unlink and truncate.

**Corrected during verification.** Nothing material. Two minor corrections: (a) the leak arithmetic is understated — deleting a 5 MiB file leaks 246 blocks ≈ 984 KiB, not "roughly 800 KiB"; (b) severity is overstated at high. ### targeted_flush_request unconditionally heap-allocates a 2 KiB target list and, on allocation failure, warns and returns Ok(()) with the remote half skipped — but the frame-reuse LUF drain covers the general free path, the process cpumask holds only currently-running CPUs, and two of the three cited callers free nothing, so the residual hazard is narrow (kernel-OOM + LUF-suppressed exec stack reset

**Trigger.** Requires a kernel-heap (2048-byte slab class) allocation failure at the instant of a targeted shootdown. Concretely: a multithreaded process with threads running on >=2 CPUs; drive the kernel heap to exhaustion from userland (fork/mmap/memfd churn), then have one thread `execve` — `process_vm_reset_stack` sets `suppress_cross_cpu_drain`, gather-unmaps the old stack into `gathered`, and calls `tlb::flush_all_for_process`; if `KVec::<usize>::zeroed

**Evidence.**

- slopos-ostd/src/cpu/x86_64/pcr.rs:33 — `pub const MAX_CPUS: usize = 256;` — the allocation is 2048 bytes on every targeted flush
- mm/src/tlb.rs:887-895 — the local flush happens before this, so 'falling back to local' means the *remote* half is skipped and `Ok(())` is returned

**Prior art.** Linux passes `struct cpumask *` (a 32-byte bitmap for NR_CPUS=256) by reference through `flush_tlb_multi`; there is no allocation anywhere on the shootdown path, and `on_each_cpu_mask` takes the mask. A shootdown that can fail to send is not a failure mode the design admits.

### The neighbor cache allocates on the heap under its IRQ-disabling SpinLock on both the ARP RX and IPv4 TX paths, and turns a fallible allocation into a kernel panic there (`.expect("neighbor: alloc")`, plus two `collect()`s that panic via `KVec::from_iter`). If the kernel heap cannot satisfy that ~4*sizeof(PacketBuf) request — either true exhaustion or the transient 

**Evidence.**
- net/src/neighbor.rs:176-186 — `NeighborCacheInner { entries: KVec::new(), … }` inside `SpinLock::new(..., LOCK_LEVEL_REGISTRY)`; the vector starts with zero capacity and is never reserved
- net/src/neighbor.rs:245 — `let mut inner = self.inner.lock();` at the top of `insert_or_update`
- net/src/neighbor.rs:305 — `let _ = inner.entries.push(NeighborEntry { … });` — a growing `KVec` push, i.e. a buddy/slab allocation, under that lock

**Prior art.** Linux's `neigh_alloc` uses a preallocated slab cache and explicitly does the allocation outside `tbl->lock`; the `unres_qlen_bytes` accounting bounds the pending queue without allocating under the lock. FreeBSD's `llentry` allocation likewise happens before `IF_AFDATA_WLOCK`.
