### fork COW-marks pages held by a live PinnedUserBuffer (SlopRing registered/provided buffers, TCP MSG_ZEROCOPY), and because the pin itself keeps the MetaSlot refcount >= 2 the parent's next write always takes resolve_multi_ref and moves the parent off the pinned frame — a silent zero-copy data-integrity break, not a memory-safety hole and not a privilege-boundary leak

`mm crate` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Unprivileged userland, production kernel: (1) `p = mmap(NULL, 4096, PROT_READ|PROT_WRITE, MAP_PRIVATE|MAP_ANONYMOUS)` and touch it so the page is present (PinnedUserBuffer::pin rejects NotPresent). (2) `fd = ring_setup(...)`; `syscall(161 /*SYSCALL_RING_REGISTER*/, fd, 2 /*RING_REGISTER_BUFFERS*/, &iov{addr=p,len=4096}, 1)` — ring/src/buffers.rs:228 pins the page, MetaSlot refcount 1→2. (3) `syscall(72 /*fork*/)` — clone_cow_snapshot_parent marks

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:2708-2739 — `clone_cow_snapshot_parent` marks every writable USER page COW via `ostd_mark_cow_4kb` with no consultation of the frame's pin state or reference count
- mm/src/pinned_user_buffer.rs:81-122 — `PinnedUserBuffer::pin` takes an owning `UFrame<AnonymousMeta>` ref per page via `wrap_user_paddr` and holds it for the buffer's lifetime
- mm/src/cow.rs:40-46 — the fault-side decision: `if ref_count <= 1 { resolve_single_ref } else { resolve_multi_ref }` — a pin guarantees `ref_count > 1`, so the faulting side always copies

**Fix.** During `clone_cow_snapshot_parent`, check the frame's reference count (or a `pinned` bit on the frame metadata) before marking COW; if the frame carries a reference beyond its PTE count, copy it eagerly into a fresh frame for the child and leave the parent's PTE writable and pointing at the pinned original. Add a `utest!` that registers a ring buffer, forks, writes in the parent, and asserts the ring still sees the parent's writes.

**Prior art.** Linux post-CVE-2020-29374: `pin_user_pages`/`FOLL_PIN` is distinct from `get_user_pages`, `folio_maybe_dma_pinned()` gates `copy_present_page` into an eager copy at fork time, and `PageAnonExclusive` tracks exclusive ownership. The ASPLOS'23 'Copy-on-Pin' paper is the full treatment. Zircon sidesteps it by making pins

(1) It is not a meaningful cross-process data leak: the pin prevents any use-after-free, nothing is corrupted, and the only other party that can observe the detached frame is the process's own fork child, which already inherited that memory and runs at identical privilege; if the child execs (the normal case) its mapping is gone and there is no second reader at all. Let the first frame's force_full_redraw (mod.rs:220) pass. (2) Every frame thereafter: on surfaces 0..15 send 8x SurfaceDamage(x=0, y=2k, w=1920, h=1); on surfaces 16..31 send 8x

- userland/src/apps/compositor/protocol.rs:539-572 — `handle_surface_damage` stores up to `MAX_PENDING_DAMAGE = 8` (protocol.rs:24) raw client rects per surface with **no clamping to the surface, the output, or any sane range**; the rect is built as `x1: x + w -
- userland/src/apps/compositor/mod.rs:558-568 — `add_window_damage` adds each committed client rect to `output_damage` translated by `window.x/window.y`, again unclamped.
- userland/src/apps/compositor/region.rs:63-69 — `Region::add` calls `self.subtract(&rect)` before pushing, and `subtract_into` (:174-215) shatters each overlapped stored rect into up to four remainder rects. Interleaved horizontal and vertical stripes therefore

**Prior art.** Every serious compositor uses a banded (y-then-x) region representation — `pixman_region32_t` in X/Wayland, `wl_region`/`pixman` in weston and wlroots — where union, intersection and subtraction are all a single linear merge over sorted bands, so a region's rect count is bounded by the band structure rather than by fra

(1) 'no clamping' is imprecise: the >MAX_PENDING_DAMAGE overflow branch at protocol.rs:563-568 DOES clamp to the surface with saturating arithmetic — only the first 8 rects per surface are unclamped, which is exactly the count the attack uses, so the defect stands. `build system, CI gates, test infrastructure, verification, docs` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** No runtime trigger exists — neither gate is kernel code. The trigger is an environment/toolchain condition at build time. - Reproduced: `bash -c 'set -euo pipefail; OBJDUMP=definitely_not_a_real_objdump_xyz; COUNT="$("$OBJDUMP" -d ... 2>/dev/null | grep -cE ... || true)"; ...'` → COUNT=0 → the OK branch. The `2>/dev/null` swallows "command not found" and `|| true` swallows the non-

In `check_stack_sizes.sh`, `exit 2` when `llvm-readobj --stack-sizes` produces zero `Size:` records, and additionally compare the entry count against a floor (44,219 today) so a partial section is caught too. - slopos-ostd/src/mm/hhdm_bytes.rs:43,68,93,112,126,141 — `pub fn write_bytes(virt: VirtAddr, offset, src)`, `read_unaligned<T: Copy>(virt, offset)`, `copy_page(src, dst)` etc.; only a null check and a 4 KiB bound; module doc :18-31 states the real preconditions

**Fix.** Split the surface in three. (1) Anything that takes a bare `*mut T`/`u64` VA/`VirtAddr` and dereferences it becomes `unsafe fn` with a `# Safety` section: `ptr_buf::{with_buf_mut, with_buf, write_kernel_va, zero_bytes_at_kernel_va, copy_bytes, ptr_add, install_buf_mut, nullable_write, write_if_non_null, write_at_index, copy_with_nul_terminator}`, all of `hhdm_bytes`, all of `memory`. Consumers then either write `unsafe` (tripping the gate and for

**Prior art.** Rust-for-Linux's coding guidelines make the distinction load-bearing: `# Safety` on an `unsafe fn` states the obligation, `// SAFETY:` at a call site discharges it — a function with preconditions the type system cannot express is `unsafe fn`, full stop, and a missing/hand-wavy SAFETY comment is a blocking review commen

**Corrected during verification.** Nothing factual about the code — every quoted line checks out. What is wrong is the classification and severity. The finding is filed as a critical *security vulnerability* with confidence 93, but it has no attacker trigger: its stated exploit ("a single line in any forbid(unsafe_code) crate") is a kernel source edit, not an input. I walked every production consumer of the five surfaces and none t

### The ext2 VFS adapter casts the u64 VFS offset to u32 on read/write, so offsets >= 2^32 silently alias onto the same inode's low bytes — a real POSIX-conformance/silent-corruption bug, but it crosses no privilege boundary and is not an information-disclosure primitive

`fs crate` · confirmed with corrections; 2 verification lenses

**Trigger.** On a default QEMU boot (virtio-disk0 attached per scripts/qemu_run.sh:384, ext2 mounted per boot/src/boot_services.rs:133-152), from any unprivileged userland process, against a file on the ext2 mount ("/mnt/f" on the RAM-root path, "/f" on the virtio-root path): fd = open("/mnt/f", O_RDWR|O_CREAT); write(fd, "AAAA", 4); lseek(fd, 4294967296, SEEK_SET) -> returns 4294967296 (fdops.rs:396-408 accepts it); write(fd, "X", 1) -> returns 1, and byte 0

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/ext2_vfs.rs:199 — `fn read(&self, inode, offset: u64, buf) { self.with_ext2(|fs| fs.read_file(inode as u32, offset as u32, buf)) }`
- fs/src/ext2_vfs.rs:203 — `fn write(&self, inode, offset: u64, buf) { ... fs.write_file(inode as u32, offset as u32, buf) }` — both `as u32` casts silently discard bits 32..63
- fs/src/fileio/fdops.rs:396-409 — `file_seek_fd` accepts any `new_pos >= 0` up to `i64::MAX` and stores it into `open_file.position`

**Fix.** Give `Ext2Fs` an explicit `max_file_size` (derived from `block_size` and the indirect reach, capped at `u32::MAX`), and in `ext2_vfs.rs` return `VfsError::InvalidArgument`/`EFBIG` when `offset > max_file_size` or `offset + buf.len() > max_file_size` instead of casting. Better: change `Ext2Fs::read_file`/`write_file` to take `u64` and do the bound check where the geometry is known. Add a `stest!` that lseeks past 4 GiB and asserts the write fails 

**Prior art.** Linux's VFS carries `loff_t` (signed 64-bit) end to end and each filesystem publishes `s_maxbytes`; `generic_write_checks()` rejects a write past `s_maxbytes` with `-EFBIG` and `rw_verify_area()` rejects a negative or overflowing `pos` with `-EINVAL`. For ext2/rev0 the correct maximum is derived from the block-size-dep

**Corrected during verification.** Three things. (1) Severity: labelled "critical" and described as an "information-disclosure primitive" — it is neither. fs/src/ext2/file.rs:20-26 confines the alias to the same inode, so a 4 GiB read returns the start of the file the caller already opened for reading, and a 4 GiB write clobbers byte 0 of the file the caller already holds writable. No privilege boundary is crossed and the attacker 

### ramfs silently truncates directory-entry names to 32 bytes instead of returning ENAMETOOLONG: a >32-byte name is stored under (and only reachable as) its 32-byte prefix, so repeat O_CREAT of the same long name allocates a fresh inode each time and a long name can squat a legitimate short name — but the entries are listed by readdir and reclaimable by unlinking the truncated name, so no permanent l

`fs crate` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Concrete and real for the correctness half: from any userland process, `open("/tmp/" + "A"*40, O_CREAT|O_WRONLY)` succeeds and returns a usable fd, but `open("/tmp/" + "A"*40, O_RDONLY)` afterwards returns ENOENT, and each repeat of the O_CREAT call allocates a fresh inode (duplicate check at ramfs/mod.rs:78 can never fire for a 40-byte name). Squatting variant: `open("/tmp/" + "A"*32 + "X", O_CREAT)` then `open("/tmp/" + "A"*32, O_CREAT|O_EXCL)`

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/ramfs/mod.rs:84-86 — `let len = name.len().min(MAX_NAME_LEN); entry.name[..len].copy_from_slice(&name[..len]); entry.name_len = len;` — truncate and return Ok
- fs/src/lib.rs:5 — `pub const MAX_NAME_LEN: usize = 32;`
- fs/src/ramfs/mod.rs:78 — the duplicate check is `entry.name_len == name.len() && entry.name[..entry.name_len] == *name`, which a 40-byte query can never satisfy against a 32-byte stored name

**Fix.** Reject rather than truncate: in `RamInode::add_dir_entry`, `if name.len() > MAX_NAME_LEN { return Err(VfsError::NameTooLong) }`. Better, hoist the check into `vfs/path.rs`'s component walk and `vfs/ops.rs`'s create/mkdir/rename entry points so all three filesystems answer identically, and raise `MAX_NAME_LEN` to 255 to match ext2 and POSIX `NAME_MAX` (the 32-byte `DirEntry` array is a per-entry cost that a `KVec<u8>` name would remove). Add a `st

**Prior art.** Every real filesystem returns `ENAMETOOLONG` from `->lookup`/`->create` when `dentry->d_name.len > NAME_MAX`; Linux's `filename_lookup` enforces it centrally in `link_path_walk` so no filesystem can accept-and-truncate. Silent truncation in a namespace is a known corruption source (it is why `strlcpy`-style APIs report

**Corrected during verification.** Three things. (1) "Permanent, unreclaimable inode leak … no recovery path" is false: the truncated entry is returned by readdir/vfs_list (ramfs/mod.rs:425) and unlink of the 32-byte prefix matches at remove_dir_entry:98 and calls reset() on the inode (:393), which alloc_inode then reuses — 4096 unlinks of the prefix name fully recover the pool. (2) The DoS is not caused by this bug: RAMFS_MAX_INOD

### Four (not six) `.expect()` allocation panics in `ring/`, all unprivileged-reachable but only on genuine kernel-heap exhaustion; in a production build each is caught by the syscall recovery boundary and kills only the calling task

`abi crate, SlopRing (io_uring-like), handles, IPC, kernel-services` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Requires the attacker to already be able to exhaust the kernel heap; there is no trigger without that. Given it: process calls `ring_setup(4096, &params)` (cq_entries = 8192), submits up to 8192 deferred ops (e.g. `OP_POLL_ADD` on never-ready fds) via `ring_enter(fd, n, 0, 0)`, then blocks in `ring_enter(fd, 0, 1, 0)`. `harvest`'s loop re-enters `harvest_step` at least every 50 ms, and each pass calls `inflight.snapshot()` → `KVec::with_capacity(

**Evidence.**
- ring/src/ring_obj.rs:256 — `let mut out = KVec::with_capacity(self.rows.len()).expect("ring: inflight snapshot");` inside `InFlightVec::snapshot`
- ring/src/ring_obj.rs:258 — `out.push(r.alias()).expect("ring: inflight snapshot push");`
- ring/src/enter.rs:605-608 — `harvest_step` calls `ring.inflight.snapshot()` on every iteration of the harvest loop, i.e. at least every 50 ms per blocked `ring_enter`

**Fix.** Make all six fallible. `snapshot()` should return `Result<KVec<InFlight>, AllocError>` and `harvest_step` should treat failure as 'no progress this pass' (fall through to the block); `InFlightVec::with_capacity` and `RingRegion::paddrs` should return `Result` and propagate `-ENOMEM` out of `ring_setup`; the registry should be built once at boot rather than lazily. Better still, eliminate the per-pass snapshot entirely — walk the live table by ind

**Prior art.** Linux's rule for this class is that any allocation on a user-reachable path is `GFP_KERNEL` with a checked return; `BUG_ON`/`panic` on allocation failure is a reviewable defect. The fail-nth fault-injection framework (`/proc/self/fail-nth`) exists specifically to enumerate these unwind points deterministically — which 

**Corrected during verification.** Three things. (a) Count: only four of the six sites can allocate. `region.rs:76` and `ring_obj.rs:258` are `push`es into a vector reserved to exactly the number of pushes; `KVec::push`'s `Vec::try_reserve(1)` is a no-op when capacity is spare, so those two `.expect()`s are unreachable by construction. (b) Impact: it is not "a kernel panic any unprivileged process can request". Production boots wra

### No dcache and no dentry/inode object layer: path-based syscalls re-walk each component from the mount root via a u64-keyed, lock-contract-free FileSystem trait behind one global per-filesystem lock — a scalability/maintainability gap, not a correctness defect, and not "every syscall" (open fds cache (fs, inode))

`fs crate` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** No failure trigger exists — nothing here computes a wrong result. The cost behaviour is trivially observable: `open("/usr/share/fonts/x.psf", O_RDONLY)` from userland reaches `fs/src/fileio/fdops.rs:176` → `vfs_open_handle_flags` → `vfs_open_flags` → `resolve_path`, which issues four `fs.lookup` calls, each taking `CACHED_EXT2.lock()` twice-over (one lock per `with_fs`, two `read_inode_num` parses inside it), followed by one `fs.stat`. Under the 

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/vfs/traits.rs:191-338 — the entire `FileSystem` trait: every method is keyed by `InodeId` (a bare `u64`, :8) and takes `&self` with no documented exclusion contract
- fs/src/vfs/path.rs:9-38 — `resolve_path` walks every component from the mount root on every call, issuing one `fs.lookup` per component with no caching of any kind
- fs/src/vfs/ops.rs:59-99 — `vfs_open_flags` calls `resolve_path` (a full walk), then `fs.stat` (a second lookup of the same inode), then possibly `resolve_parent` (a *third* full walk of the parent path)

**Fix.** Land it in stages. First, write the exclusion contract for `FileSystem` — for each method, which locks the VFS holds and which the implementor must take — because that is free and immediately catches the two bugs above. Second, interpose a refcounted `Inode` object (`KArc<Inode>` with a generation) so identity is pinned by a handle rather than a number; that alone fixes the recycle bug and gives you somewhere to hang a per-inode lock. Third, add 

**Prior art.** Linux's dcache is the canonical answer: a hash of `(parent dentry, name)` → dentry, with RCU-walk making the common case entirely store-free (validated by per-dentry `d_seq` + a single `mount_lock` sample) and falling back to REF-walk on any complication. FreeBSD's namecache plus the per-`VOP_` documented entry/exit lo

**Corrected during verification.** Four concrete errors plus a framing error. (1) The title's "on every syscall" is false — `fs/src/vfs_file_ops.rs:39` resolves an open fd to a stored `(fs, inode)` pair, so only path-based syscalls walk. (2) "`vfs_open_flags` … a *third* full walk" is false: at most two walks, and `resolve_parent` runs only on the NotFound+create branch, never on a successful open; the `fs.stat` counted as a "secon

### Five in-tree comments assert a "close-on-fork" ring-fd policy that does not exist (ring fds are inherited by fork and survive exec), but every use of the fd is gated by registry::owner_is, so the defect is comment/doc drift plus ordinary inherited-fd resource retention — not an authorization or memory-safety hole

`abi crate, SlopRing (io_uring-like), handles, IPC, kernel-services` · confirmed with corrections

Exec survival: `int fd = ring_setup(64, &params); execve("/bin/sh", ...)` — fileio_close_on_exec (core/src/exec/mod.rs:486) skips it because cloexec == false,

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:2700-2702 — "SlopRing regions are not inherited (close-on-fork, SLOPRING § 14)"
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:2934-2937 — "SlopRing regions are NOT inherited: the child's ring fd is close-on-fork (SLOPRING § 14), and the SQ/CQ is SPSC, so a second producer in the child is forbidden by construction."
- ring/src/registry.rs:9-11 — "`ring_enter` rejects a ring entered from a process other than its creator (defence in depth on top of the close-on-fork fd policy, SLOPRING § 14)"

**Fix.** Either (a) make the claim true — add a `FileKind::Ring` (or a new `close_on_fork` FdEntry bit) skip in `fileio_clone_table_for_process`, and pass `O_CLOEXEC` when opening the ring fd; or (b) delete all four comments and re-document `owner_pid` as the primary, load-bearing authorization check. Do not leave a documented mechanism that the code does not implement. Independently, set O_CLOEXEC on ring fds to match io_uring.

**Prior art.** io_uring sets `O_RDWR|O_CLOEXEC` on the ring fd at creation (`io_uring_get_file` → `anon_inode_getfile` with O_CLOEXEC) precisely so a ring never survives exec into an unrelated program, and binds the ring to `ctx->submitter_task` at creation under IORING_SETUP_SINGLE_ISSUER after the lazily-sampled owner check proved 

**Corrected during verification.** Three things. (1) Severity: rated "high" when no authorization or safety boundary is crossed — `registry::owner_is` gates ring_enter and all four ring_register ops, and the child additionally gets no ring VMA (process_vm.rs:2939) and gets EINVAL/POLLNVAL on read/write/poll, so an inherited ring fd is completely inert in the child. The comments' *conclusions* are true; only their stated justificati

### SlopRing is a deliberate caller-as-waiter (poll/select-shaped) ring: a would-blocked row is re-probed only inside a blocking `ring_enter(min_complete > 0)`. The mechanics are exactly as described, but this is a documented, ABI-contracted, test-encoded design property — not a defect — and the finding's latency and deadlock claims are wrong.

`abi crate, SlopRing (io_uring-like), handles, IPC, kernel-services` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** `ring_setup(8)` → push an SQE that must block (e.g. OP_RECVFROM on a bound-but-idle UDP socket, or OP_READ on an empty pipe) → `ring_enter(fd, 1, 0, 0)`. `submit()` records the row via `Outcome::WouldBlock` → `ring.inflight.push(...)` (enter.rs:380-385) and returns 1; no `harvest` runs. Userspace can then spin on the CQ forever and never see a CQE — including across further `ring_enter(fd, 0, 0, 0)` calls, since re-probing is gated entirely on `m

**Evidence.**
- ring/src/enter.rs:240-248 — `if min_complete > 0 { let rc = harvest(...) }`; with `min_complete == 0` `ring_enter` returns immediately and no in-flight row is ever re-probed
- ring/src/enter.rs:479-535 — `harvest` is a loop that runs *on the calling task*: register on each file's wait queue, re-probe under the ring lock, `block_current_task_with_timeout`, repeat
- ring/src/ring_obj.rs:14-17 — "One armed-but-incomplete SQE — plain data, never a suspended future"

**Fix.** Two viable directions, and the choice should be recorded. (a) Own the position: rename/document SlopRing as a batched-submission readiness ring, and add an explicit `SLOPRING_FEAT_*` bit stating that no op completes outside `ring_enter`, so runtimes stop assuming otherwise. (b) Add the minimum async substrate: a bounded per-ring kernel worker that owns re-probing of parked rows and posts CQEs independently, plus a wake path from the file's wait q

**Prior art.** io_uring runs blocking work on `io-wq` bounded/unbounded worker pools, defers completion posting to `task_work` (with IORING_SETUP_DEFER_TASKRUN to batch it at wait time), offers SQPOLL for syscall-free submission, and expresses dependencies with IOSQE_IO_LINK/IO_DRAIN + LINK_TIMEOUT. Windows IOCP is the same completio

(1) It treats a documented, deliberately chosen design as an undocumented gap: its own evidence items 3 and 4 ARE the documentation, and the same statement appears in slopos-ostd/src/tx_reclaim.rs:11, slopos-rt/src/slopfut/reactor.rs:4-6, and the userland API doc at slopos-rt/src/ring.rs:208-211 — so recommendation (a) is already implemented except for a feature bit. `boot, early init, SMP bringup, panic/abort, link.ld, kernel binary` · confirmed with corrections

- mm/src/paging_defs.rs:62 — `const KERNEL_RW = Self::PRESENT.bits() | Self::WRITABLE.bits();` — no NO_EXECUTE, so these leaves are writable *and* executable
- boot/src/uefi_runtime.rs:93-103 — each page is mapped twice: `map_page_4kb(VirtAddr::new(phys), PhysAddr::new(phys), flags)` (identity, i.e. a low-half VA in the kernel master PML4) and again at `phys + hhdm_offset`

### The 256-slot ring registry is global with no per-process quota, but the 32-entry per-process fd table caps one process at ~29 rings — exhausting the registry needs ≥9 cooperating processes, and in a kernel with no privilege model at all this is a recoverable local resource-exhaustion nuisance, not a high-severity DoS

`abi crate, SlopRing (io_uring-like), handles, IPC, kernel-services` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Spawn 9+ processes, each doing `for (i=0;i<29;i++) ring_setup(1, &params);` then blocking forever (they must stay alive and keep the fds open). After ~256 successful rings across all of them, any further `ring_setup` in any process returns -ENOMEM; a compositor started at that moment would hit `Ring::setup(64).expect(...)` and panic. Killing or exiting any hogging process immediately frees its slots (fileio_destroy_table_for_process → RingBacking

**Evidence.**
- ring/src/registry.rs:19-22 — `const MAX_RINGS: usize = 256;` with the comment "Maximum concurrent rings system-wide"
- ring/src/registry.rs:55 — `static REGISTRY: SpinLock<Option<HandleTable<RingSlot>>>` — one global table, no per-pid partitioning
- ring/src/registry.rs:76-79 — `insert` returns `None` when `HandleTable::insert` yields `HandleError::Full`

**Fix.** Add a per-process ring count (e.g. 16 by default, checked in `registry::insert` against `owner_pid`) so the global 256 becomes a ceiling rather than a first-come-first-served pool; return `-EMFILE` on the per-process limit and keep `-ENOMEM` for the global one so the two are distinguishable. Longer term, drop the global table entirely and hang rings off the process VM slot, which already has a per-process lifetime.

**Prior art.** io_uring has no global ring cap at all: rings are bounded per-process by RLIMIT_NOFILE (each ring is an fd) and per-user by RLIMIT_MEMLOCK on the ring memory, so one user cannot starve another. Linux's general rule is that a shared kernel table either has a per-credential quota (`RLIMIT_*`, `/proc/sys/fs/file-max` plus

**Corrected during verification.** Three errors. (1) The trigger is impossible as written: `for (int i=0;i<256;i++) ring_setup(...)` cannot pin 256 slots because each ring needs an fd and the per-process fd table is a fixed 32 entries (fs/src/fileio/mod.rs:25); the loop starts failing near i=29 and each failed fd install rolls the registry entry back, so ~227 slots remain for other processes. Exhaustion requires ≥9 cooperating proc

### slibc never reclaims a detached or never-joined thread's 2 MiB stack VMA or its TCB/TLS block (mechanism confirmed), but the impact is far smaller than claimed: the mapping is lazy, the mmap window is ~128 TiB, the TCB is ~640 B of heap (not a dlmalloc segment), and nothing in the shipped userland spawns threads today

`userland platform` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Any of: (a) `pthread_create` with an attr whose `detach_state == PTHREAD_CREATE_DETACHED` — the thread runs, hits create.rs:38 `_exit(0)`, and its 2 MiB anon VMA plus its `tls_size + 640`-byte heap block stay mapped/allocated until the process exits; (b) `pthread_detach(t)` on a still-running thread — join.rs:61 reads a nonzero `child_tid`, so the free branch is skipped and nothing ever runs it again; (c) `let _ = std::thread::spawn(|| ...);` — `

**Evidence.**
- slibc/src/thread/create.rs:27-39 — `thread_trampoline` runs the start routine, runs key destructors, and calls `_exit(0)`. It never `munmap`s `(*tcb).stack_base` and never frees the TCB (it cannot: it is running on that stack).
- slibc/src/thread/join.rs:52-70 — `pthread_detach` sets `detached = true`, and frees the stack + TCB *only* if `child_tid` already reads 0 (thread already dead). For a still-running thread it returns having arranged nothing; nobody frees it later.
- slibc/src/thread/join.rs:11-49 — `pthread_join` is the only path that `munmap`s the stack and `malloc::dealloc`s the TCB.

### slibc's C stdio keeps no open-stream list, so fflush(NULL)/exit() flush only stdout+stderr — a real C11 §7.22.4.4 gap, but latent: nothing in the tree calls fopen, slibc ships rlib-only with no C consumers, and the Rust userland's file/stdout/exit paths all bypass C stdio

`userland platform` · confirmed with corrections

**Trigger.** Requires a C (or Rust-extern) program linked against slibc that calls `fopen(path, "w")`, writes fewer than BUFSIZ=4096 bytes via fwrite/fprintf, and then calls `exit()` or returns from main without `fclose` — the tail stays in the malloc'd FILE buffer and is discarded at _exit. No such program exists in the repo: there are zero callers of fopen anywhere, no .c sources, and slibc is built as an rlib only, so the trigger is currently unconstructib

**Evidence.**
- slibc/src/stdio/file.rs:287-300 — `fflush(NULL)` explicitly flushes `stdout_file()` and `stderr_file()` and returns; there is no registry of open streams to walk.
- slibc/src/stdio/file.rs:40-66 — `fopen` `malloc`s a fresh `FILE` and returns it; the FILE is never linked into any list.
- slibc/src/process/mod.rs:147-153 — `exit()` calls `fflush(ptr::null_mut())` (i.e. `fflush(NULL)`), runs atexit handlers, then `_exit`.

**Fix.** Add an intrusive singly-linked open-stream list in slibc/src/stdio/, linked on `fopen`/`fdopen` and unlinked on `fclose`, and make `fflush(NULL)` and a new `__stdio_exit` (called from `exit`) walk it. The list needs the same lock as the per-stream locking added for the thread-safety finding, so land the two together.

**Prior art.** glibc chains every FILE onto `_IO_list_all` and `_IO_cleanup()` (registered via `__libc_atexit`) walks it, flushing and closing. musl keeps `__ofl_head` under `__ofl_lock()` and `__stdio_exit` walks the same list. Both also use that list to implement `fflush(NULL)` per the standard.

**Corrected during verification.** Reachability and therefore severity. The auditor asserts this is "on the exact path a shell builtin or an app config writer takes" — it is not. Nothing in the repository calls fopen/fdopen/fclose/fflush (grep returns only slibc's own definitions), slibc is rlib-only with no C sources or C toolchain in the tree, and the actual userland goes through the std port: files via std_pal/fs/slopos.rs `File

### No exit_group syscall exists: exit()/abort()/fatal-signal terminate only the calling task, so sibling threads keep running with an intact address space and fd table — a POSIX semantic gap, not a teardown/reaping hazard, and unreachable from any shipping in-tree program (only userland test binaries create threads, and they join them)

### slibc's C stdio has no per-stream lock and no flockfile family (a real POSIX-conformance gap), but no in-tree program can hit it: every userland binary is Rust and prints through std, whose SlopOS PAL writes straight to fd 1/2 and never touches the FILE buffer; nothing outside slibc calls printf/fwrite at all, and the claimed "uninitialized bytes written to fd 1" is impossible because FILE::buf is

**Trigger.** No trigger exists in the current tree. To reach it someone would have to add code that calls the C-ABI stdio entry points (printf/fwrite/fputs/putchar) on a shared FILE from two pthreads — e.g. link a C program against slibc, or write Rust that declares `extern "C" { fn printf(..) }` and calls it from std::thread::spawn'd threads. Even then, stdout (Line) and stderr (None) can only produce interleaved/stale output or an index panic; the out-of-bo

**Evidence.**
- slibc/src/stdio/streams.rs:12-14 — `static mut STDOUT_FILE`/`STDERR_FILE`/`STDIN_FILE`, exposed as raw `*mut FILE` through the `stdout`/`stderr`/`stdin` no_mangle statics (:18-24).
- Repository grep for `flockfile`, `funlockfile`, `ftrylockfile` across slibc/ returns nothing; there is no lock field on `FILE` (slibc/src/stdio/mod.rs:74-89) and no lock acquisition anywhere in slibc/src/stdio/.
- slibc/src/stdio/file.rs:194-235 — `fwrite` does a read-modify-write of `f.buf_pos` and `f.buf[..]` with no synchronization; `flush_write_buf` (stdio/mod.rs:117-142) resets `buf_pos = 0` after the write loop.

**Fix.** Add a recursive lock word to `FILE` (a futex-backed count+owner, reusing the pthread_mutex primitives once `kind` is honored), take it at the top of every public stdio entry point, and export `flockfile`/`funlockfile`/`ftrylockfile` plus `*_unlocked` variants. Gate the acquisition on a `threads_started` global so single-threaded programs pay one predictable branch, as musl does.

**Prior art.** POSIX.1 mandates implicit locking on all stdio calls plus the `flockfile`/`funlockfile`/`getc_unlocked` family for callers that want to amortize it. musl carries a `lock` field per FILE with `FLOCK(f)`/`FUNLOCK(f)` macros that compile out entirely in a single-threaded program (`libc.threads_minus_1 == 0` fast path), so

**Corrected during verification.** Three things. (a) Reachability: the auditor asserted that shipping pthread_create plus unlocked stdio means threaded programs corrupt stdout, but never checked how in-tree programs actually print. Rust std's SlopOS PAL (slibc/std_pal/stdio/slopos.rs:65-73) implements Stdout/Stderr as direct `write(1|2, ...)` syscalls that never touch the FILE buffer, and std layers its own ReentrantLock above that

CPU B's watchdog monitor sends an NMI; vector 2 has no IST (it is absent from IST_CONFIGS), so nmi_watchdog_handler runs further down IST4. Its %rbp chain walk calls read_volatile_canonical_kernel_u64 on a canonical, 8-byte-aligned but

## Completeness critique — what a per-subsystem sweep structurally cannot see


### Lens: Architectural trajectory — where this design is heading and what breaks first as CPUs, code, and workload grow

#### REGRET #1: process/task/CPU identity is resolved by linear scan of fixed static arrays, in every subsystem independently — cost per operation is O(MAX_*), not O(live)

*high, confidence 92.* Each subsystem auditor saw its own array and judged it locally reasonable (mm sees PROCESS_VMS[256], fs sees PROCESS_TABLES[256], sched sees the registry spine, ostd sees PCR[256]). The finding is the *sum*: there is no `struct process` that owns pointers to its parts, so every subsystem re-derives identity by search, and the searches compose multiplicatively on the hottest paths. Only fs's version was reported (#180

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:129-133 — `static PROCESS_VMS: [SpinLock<ProcessVm>; MAX_PROCESSES]`, MAX_PROCESSES=256 (mm/src/memory_layout_defs.rs:370)
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:274-284 — `find_slot_for_pid` is a `for i in 0..MAX_PROCESSES` lock-free scan; 23 call sites in that file alone
- mm/src/page_fault.rs:21,27,44,47 — one user fault calls `process_vm_with_vm_space` (COW probe), `process_vm_with_vm_space` (COW handle), `is_demand_fault`→`process_vm_get_region`, and `process_vm_with_vm_space_and_region`: four independent 256-slot scans + fou

**Fix.** Introduce one owning `Process` object (KArc, generation-stamped) that holds `vm_space`, `fd_table`, `tlb_info`, `session` by reference, and hang it off the task. Resolve pid→Process exactly once, at the syscall/fault entry, and pass the reference down; delete `find_slot_for_pid` and its three siblings. Second: stop dimensioning statics by MAX_CPUS — allocate per-CPU areas at AP bring-up from the buddy so a 4-CPU machine pays for 4. Doing this now

**Prior art.** Linux: `struct task_struct` holds `mm`, `files`, `fs`, `signal` by pointer — pid lookup happens once via the pid radix tree/IDR, never on the fault path; `find_vma` is a per-mm maple tree, not a global scan. Per-CPU areas are dynamically allocated (`percpu` allocator, Tejun Heo 2009) precisely to kill the NR_CPUS-sized

#### RCU reclamation runs on CPU 0's idle loop only, and the grace period is a sequential per-CPU busy-wait — memory reclaim stops entirely whenever CPU 0 is busy, and gets slower with every CPU added

*high, confidence 90.* The sync auditor read rcu.rs and checked its internal correctness (#233 found the QS-IPI escape hatch, #224 found the infallible alloc). The disqualifying fact is in a different crate: the single line in the scheduler's idle loop that decides *who* drains. Nobody joined `rcu_process_callbacks`'s doc ("idle task, kernel thread") to its one production caller.

**Evidence.**
- sched/src/runtime.rs:246-248 — `if cpu_id == 0 { slopos_ostd::sync::rcu_process_callbacks(); }` inside `unified_idle_loop`. This is the only production caller (the other four are tests)
- Therefore: if CPU 0 is running any task at all, no RCU callback is ever invoked, on any CPU. There is no timer-driven fallback — slopos-ostd/src/sync/rcu.rs:476 `rcu_raise_softirq` only sets a flag; nothing consumes it except the CPU-0 idle path
- slopos-ostd/src/sync/rcu.rs:305-320 — callbacks live on one global Treiber stack (`PENDING_HEAD`), not per-CPU lists. Every `call_rcu` from every CPU CASes the same cacheline

**Fix.** Split reclamation from the grace period. (1) Per-CPU callback lists (`rcu_data`-shaped), drained by that CPU — kill the global Treiber stack. (2) Drain from a per-CPU worker (see the deferred-work gap) or from the timer tick's process-context tail, not from idle, so callbacks run under load. (3) Replace the caller-spinning grace period with a counter-based state machine: a global grace-period counter, per-CPU last-seen counters advanced at the qu

**Prior art.** Linux Tree RCU (Paul McKenney): per-CPU `rcu_data` callback lists, a hierarchical `rcu_node` tree so grace-period detection is O(log N) not O(N) serial, `RCU_SOFTIRQ`/`rcuc` kthreads to invoke callbacks, `rcu_nocbs` to offload them off the CPU entirely, and `synchronize_rcu` sleeping on a completion. `call_rcu` never s

#### The entire VM concurrency model is one interrupt-disabling spinlock per process, taken and dropped four times per page fault — there is no reader/writer split, no per-VMA lock, and no rmap to make one possible

*high, confidence 90.* The mm auditor reported consequences one at a time — #186 (O(n) gap search under the lock), #117 (four acquisitions from the #PF IST handler), #131 (allocation under the lock), #133 (million-iteration spin), #137 (no rmap). Each was scored as a local defect. They are five symptoms of a single choice: the address space is protected by a cli-spinlock, which forbids sleeping, forbids allocation, forbids IPI rendezvous, 

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:129-133 — `static PROCESS_VMS: [SpinLock<ProcessVm>; MAX_PROCESSES]`, `LOCK_LEVEL_RESOURCE`. `SpinLock` in this kernel is an IRQ-disabling lock
- mm/src/page_fault.rs:21-52 — the fault path acquires and releases it four separate times (COW probe, COW handle, `is_demand_fault`, `handle_demand_fault`), so the VMA lookup that authorises the fault is not atomic with the mapping that satisfies it
- mm/src/demand.rs:75 — `handle_demand_fault` calls `alloc_kernel_page()` *while the caller holds the lock*, and mm/src/page_alloc/buddy.rs:1064 shows that path reaching `luf::drain_if_reusing_frame`, which does an IRQs-on cross-CPU IPI rendezvous. The lock's co

(1) Change the lock class first: make the address-space lock a sleeping/preemptible rwlock (`PreemptMutex`-shaped, which the codebase already has at mm/src/mmu/luf.rs:212), not a cli-spinlock. That single change legalises allocation, IPI rendezvous, and blocking I/O under it and dissolves #128/#131/#133 as a class rather than one at a time. It requires proving no IRQ handler takes it — #117 says the #PF IST diagnostic path do

**Prior art.** Linux's trajectory is the exact roadmap: BKL → `mmap_sem` (a sleeping rwsem, never a spinlock — a spinlock was never even considered because faults do I/O) → `mmap_lock` rename → per-VMA locks with SPF (Suren Baghdasaryan, 6.4) once maple tree + rmap made it possible. #### REGRET #3: there is no deferred-work substrate — the model is one dedicated kernel thread per work source, plus a global idle-loop callback list, and neither scales in CPUs or in work sources

*high, confidence 88.* This is an absent mechanism, so no file expresses it. The ring auditor found the local symptom (#41: SlopRing has no kernel-side async execution) and concluded it was a ring problem. It is not — SlopRing has nowhere to defer to. `sched/src/kthread.rs` exists (72 lines, a full spawn/join API) and has **zero callers**, which is the tell: the generic mechanism was written, found unusable, and bypassed.

- Each is a *singleton*, not per-CPU: drivers/src/virtio_net.rs:204 `static NAPI_WAKER` — one waker, one thread, so 100% of network RX softirq-equivalent work runs on whichever single CPU the netpoll thread lands on, regardless of core count. Adding CPUs cannot

**Prior art.** Linux pre-2010 had exactly this "one kthread per work source" model; Tejun Heo's concurrency-managed workqueue (cmwq, 2.6.36) replaced it specifically because the thread count and per-thread stack cost blew up and none of them could share CPU concurrency. *high, confidence 85.* The mm auditor found the *absent* shootdown on munmap (#125) and the IRQ-discipline violation in the drain (#128) — both about the drain being wrong when it runs. This is about the drain never running, on a machine the code claims to support, plus the throughput shape of the drain when it does. Neither is visible from a 4-CPU test run, and MAX_CPUS lives in a different crate from the mask.

**Prior art.** Linux `cpumask_t` is sized by NR_CPUS and `cpumask_var_t` is heap-allocated above CONFIG_CPUMASK_OFFSTACK precisely to avoid this class; `mm_cpumask()` scopes shootdowns to the address space; `tlb_gather_mmu`/`tlb_finish_mmu` batch the flush so the free path never IPIs per page; lazy-TLB + `cpu_tlbstate` makes idle CPU

#### Scheduling is strict 5-level priority with FIFO within a level and no aging, no bandwidth control, and no fairness — while the set of permanently-runnable above-Normal kernel threads grows with every driver

*medium, confidence 90.* The sched auditor checked the mechanics — work stealing (#211), affinity (#215), the all-CPU lock scan (#205), the sleep queue (#212) — all of which assume the policy is a given. The policy is the trajectory risk: it is fine with 5 kernel threads and will not be at 20, and nothing in the code states the assumption.

**Evidence.**
- sched/src/per_cpu.rs:404-420 — `dequeue_highest_priority` is `for queue in &self.ready_queues { if let Some(task) = queue.dequeue() { return … } }`. Strict priority, first non-empty wins, no aging, no vruntime, no deadline
- sched/src/per_cpu.rs:56 — `NUM_PRIORITY_LEVELS: usize = 5`; sched/src/per_cpu.rs:203 `ready_queues: [ReadyQueue; 5]`, each a plain intrusive FIFO
- sched/src/per_cpu.rs:229 — the time slice is a single `AtomicU32` initialised to 10 ticks for every task at every priority. There is no per-task weight, no nice, no interactivity credit

**Fix.** Add bandwidth control before adding more KernelIo threads: a per-priority-class runtime/period budget (95%/100 ms is Linux's default) enforced at the tick, which demotes an over-budget class to Normal for the rest of the period. That converts "a buggy driver thread wedges userland" from an unbounded hang into a measurable throttle. Separately, replace FIFO-within-Normal with a weighted virtual-time queue before the desktop workload (compositor + 

**Prior art.** Linux `sched_rt_runtime_us`/`sched_rt_period_us` RT throttling exists precisely because strict priority plus a buggy RT task equals a dead machine; CFS→EEVDF for the fair class; `SCHED_DEADLINE` for the cases strict priority was standing in for. #### The TCB boundary is drawn around a crate, not around a set of invariants, and the metric that governs it is blind to the thing that actually determines trust: 1,406 safe public functions in OSTD versus 60 unsafe ones

*medium, confidence 88.* Two auditors got adjacent pieces — #236 showed the published ratio is not comparable to Asterinas's, and #90/#221/#158 each found a specific safe-fn-with-unstated-obligation. What nobody said is that those three are not independent bugs: they are the predicted, measurable output of a metric whose gradient rewards exactly that move, and the size of the affected surface was never counted.

**Evidence.**
- Measured across slopos-ostd/src: 1,406 `pub fn`, of which only 60 are `pub unsafe fn`. 645 `unsafe {` blocks, 111 `unsafe fn` total. So ~96% of the trusted crate's public API is safe — meaning ~1,350 entry points each of which is an unverified soundness assert
- The metric's actual gradient (scripts/tcb_ratio.sh:16-32): numerator = *lines containing the `unsafe` keyword* in ostd+annex; denominator = all kernel-crate LoC + annex LoC. Two perverse consequences beyond #236's. (a) Writing safe code anywhere in the kernel 
- OSTD contains 40 files / ~3,585 LoC with no `unsafe` keyword at all (measured) — klog, numfmt, string, bitmap, ring_buffer, wl_currency and friends. They are in the TCB because they are in the crate, and they dilute the denominator of any per-file trust argume

**Fix.** Change what is measured, and the boundary will follow. Publish, alongside the line ratio, the number that predicts trust: **count of safe `pub fn` in OSTD whose body contains `unsafe`** (each is an unproven soundness assertion) versus `pub unsafe fn` (each is an obligation honestly propagated). Gate that count with a ratchet — it may only go down. That single change makes #90, #221 and #158 metric-visible instead of metric-rewarded, and it makes 

**Prior art.** RustBelt / the Rust community's own framing: the unit of trust is the *safe abstraction boundary*, i.e. the safe API whose soundness is asserted, not the count of `unsafe` tokens behind it. Asterinas measures crate-granular Linked Code Size and puts the whole dependency closure of any unsafe-containing crate in the TCB

#### There is no memory-reclaim mechanism of any kind — no watermarks, no shrinker, no page-cache eviction, no OOM policy — so the fallible-allocation discipline has no counterpart that can ever make an allocation succeed

*medium, confidence 85.* An absent mechanism has no file. The mm auditor verified that allocations are fallible and the practice auditor counted them (#184: 506 fallible sites, never exercised). Both treated ENOMEM as a return value to be handled. Nobody asked the system-level question: when the answer is ENOMEM, what in this kernel is capable of changing that answer? Nothing is.

**Evidence.**
- A grep for `shrink|reclaim|evict|kswapd|pagedaemon|oom|memory.pressure` across mm/src, slopos-ostd/src/mm, fs/src and core/src returns only ACPI-memmap 'reclaimable' region types (mm/src/memory_init.rs:31,309), slab-internal page recycling, and ASID slot evict
- mm/src/page_alloc/pcp.rs:22-23 — the only 'watermarks' in the kernel are `PCP_LOW_WATERMARK: u32 = 8` / `PCP_HIGH_WATERMARK`, per-CPU cache refill thresholds. There is no zone min/low/high, so nothing can even detect pressure
- No rlimit, quota, or per-process memory accounting: grep for `rlimit|RLIMIT|mem_limit|nproc` across core/, mm/, sched/, abi/ returns nothing outside userland's libc. One process can consume all of physical memory, and #186 shows userland can create unbounded V

**Fix.** Do not build reclaim yet — build the two things reclaim needs and that are cheap now and expensive later. (1) Zone watermarks and a `memory_pressure()` predicate in the buddy so subsystems can be told to back off, and so 'we are near OOM' is observable before allocations start failing. (2) A shrinker registration interface (`fn(target_pages) -> pages_freed`) in ostd that any cache must register at construction — make it a requirement for adding a

**Prior art.** Linux: per-zone `min/low/high` watermarks, `kswapd` for background reclaim, direct reclaim in the allocator slow path, the `shrinker` API that every cache (dcache, inode, slab) registers with, and the OOM killer with `oom_score_adj` as the last resort. FreeBSD: `pagedaemon`, `vm_pageout` with the free/inactive/laundry 

**Coverage verdict.** COVERED WELL. The subsystem sweep is genuinely strong on the things a careful reader of one file can find: memory-safety soundness holes in the OSTD surface (#90, #221, #158 are excellent and mutually reinforcing), the network stack's protocol hardening gaps (the RFC 5961/793 cluster is production-grade review), the mm TLB/shootdown correctness set (#125, #127, #128, #131-133), and the process/practice gaps (#241 no fuzzing, #184 no fault injection, #236 the TCB-ratio methodology). The confidence calibration in the existing findings looks honest. If those 253 were all fixed, the kernel would be materially safer at its current size on its current 4-CPU target. STILL DARK, and this is what the per-subsystem structure cost: 1. Seams between a mechanism and its only caller. The strongest example is RCU: rcu.rs was audited thoroughly for internal correctness, but the single line in sched/src/runtime.rs:247 that makes reclamation happen only on CPU 0's idle loop was in a different crate, so nobody joined them. Same shape for LUF's u64 mask (mm/src/mmu/luf.rs:133) versus MAX_CPUS=256 (slopos-ostd/src/cpu/x86_64/pcr.rs:33) — a silent memory-corruption cliff at exactly 64 CPUs that no single file expresses. Same for the production panic-recovery boundary, which exists (core/src/syscall/user_loop.rs:101) but is switched off for the entire test suite by a predicate in a third crate (slopos-ostd/src/panic_recovery.rs:38). 2. Absent mechanisms. Nobody found the missing deferred-work substrate, the missing reclaim path, the missing scheduler bandwidth control, or the missing per-process resource accounting, because you cannot read a mechanism that is not there. The tell for all four is present in the code and was walked past: `sched/src/kthread.rs` is a complete, well-formed, zero-caller API — the generic mechanism was written, found unusable, and routed around by five bespoke singleton threads. 3. Symptom-versus-cause. Five separate mm findings (#117, #128, #131, #133, #186) are five faces of one decision: the address space is protected by an interrupt-disabling spinlock, which forbids the allocation, the IPI rendezvous and the blocking that the fault path unavoidably does.

#### There is no process-identity object and no bounded PID allocator: process_id is a free-running counter that is also a direct index into 256-entry arrays, so the 256th process ever created cannot have a single page fault resolved

*critical, confidence 88.* Each auditor found the sighting inside their own file and reported it as a local array-bounds bug: mm found the TLB table (#124), abi-ring-ipc found the wrapping allocator used as an authorization token (#48), syscall found the fd-table scan (#180), drivers-io found the 16384-entry input map (#192). None of them could see that these are one absent mechanism, because the absent thing — a Process struct with a lifecycl

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:1541-1542 — `let process_id = alloc.next_process_id; alloc.next_process_id += 1;` — monotonic, never recycled, never bounded. Same at :2874-2875 for fork.
- mm/src/memory_layout_defs.rs:370 — `pub const MAX_PROCESSES: usize = 256;` — this is a *slot* count (create_process_vm scans PROCESS_VMS for a free slot at :1520-1539), not an id range.
- mm/src/page_fault.rs:13 — `if process_id == INVALID_PROCESS_ID || (process_id as usize) >= MAX_PROCESSES { return false; }` — try_resolve_user_fault refuses to resolve *any* fault for a process whose id has passed 256. Every demand fault and every CoW fault fo

**Fix.** Decide what a process id *is* and write it down once. Either (a) make it a dense recycled slot index with a generation counter packed in the high bits (pid = slot | gen<<16), which fixes the direct-index sites and #48's stale-token aliasing in one move, or (b) keep sparse monotonic ids and make every consumer a map, deleting the [_; MAX_PROCESSES] arrays. Until then, at minimum make create_process_vm refuse to hand out an id >= MAX_PROCESSES rath

**Prior art.** Linux separates struct pid (refcounted object) from the numeric pid (a per-namespace name allocated from an IDR with a wraparound policy and pid_max), precisely so that no subsystem may use the integer as an index or a capability; every long-lived reference holds a struct pid, and the number is resolved late. seL4 and 

#### There is no interruptible-sleep discipline: interruptibility is opt-in per call site, and pipes, waitpid and futex opted out — while the trusted crate's documentation names the pipe path as the exemplar of the discipline

*high, confidence 88.* This is the shared-assumption failure. The ERESTARTSYS/SA_RESTART machinery in core/src/syscall/dispatch.rs is complete, correct and impressive, so the syscall-boundary auditor reasonably concluded blocking syscalls are interruptible. The fs auditor read pipe_file_ops.rs and saw a textbook lost-wakeup-free wait_event with a pure predicate — correct on its own terms; a missing signal check is invisible unless you are 

**Evidence.**
- slopos-ostd/src/sync/wait_queue.rs:161-164 — "Signal-pending is intentionally **not** an outcome — callers check `has_pending_signal()` themselves after the wait returns, matching the existing SlopOS discipline used by `PipeReadOps::read`, the TTY input path, 
- fs/src/pipe_file_ops.rs — `grep -c has_pending_signal fs/src/pipe_file_ops.rs` returns 0. The three blocking waits at :216 (PipeReadOps::read — the function the doc names), :326 and :433 (write paths) have no signal check in the predicate and none after the wa
- sched/src/scheduler.rs:1846-1851 — task_wait_for (the waitpid block) is a bare `wait_event(|| parked_task_has_exited(target))`: a process in waitpid cannot be interrupted by any signal.

**Fix.** Make interruptibility a property of the primitive, not of the caller: add `WaitOutcome::Signal` and a `wait_event_interruptible` that folds the has_pending_signal() probe into the predicate (so the wait actually terminates) and reports it as an outcome, then convert pipe read/write, task_wait_for and futex_wait. The doc comment at wait_queue.rs:161 should be deleted in the same commit — it currently documents a convention the tree does not follow

**Prior art.** Linux encodes it in the task state itself — TASK_INTERRUPTIBLE vs TASK_UNINTERRUPTIBLE — so the *scheduler* decides whether a signal wakes a sleeper, and signal_wake_up needs no cooperation from the wait site; TASK_KILLABLE was added in 2.6.25 specifically because per-site opt-in kept being forgotten. **Prior art.** Linux resolves this by simply taking the fault: copy_to_user's page fault runs handle_mm_fault, which performs do_wp_page (CoW break) or do_anonymous_page (lazy allocation) and then resumes the copy — the extable entry exists only for genuinely bad addresses. #### No reclaim, no OOM policy, no watermarks and no reserve: memory exhaustion is unrecoverable by construction, and the unmap path needs to allocate 2 KiB to flush other CPUs' TLBs — so under pressure it silently skips the shootdown

*high, confidence 82.* An auditor can only see the allocation sites, and they did: #175 (panic on the TCP established path), #213 (infallible BTreeMap insert in waitpid), #224 (synchronize_rcu allocates infallibly), #132 (the shootdown's 2 KiB KVec), #184 (506 fallible sites never executed), #174 (fixed global object tables). What no file states is the system-level premise those all depend on — that an allocation failure is a transient con

**Evidence.**
- mm/src/page_alloc/buddy.rs — grep for watermark/min_free/reserve finds only PCP_LOW_WATERMARK / PCP_HIGH_WATERMARK (:840-1016), which are per-CPU cache refill thresholds, not zone reserves. There is no low/min watermark, no GFP_ATOMIC-equivalent reserve, no ks
- mm/src/tlb.rs:898-907 — the targeted shootdown allocates `KVec::<usize>::zeroed(MAX_CPUS)` (2 KiB) and, on failure, logs "falling back to local" and `return Ok(())` — reporting success to a caller that has just unmapped pages. The pages then go back to the bud
- mm/src/process_vm.rs:2246-2485 — process_vm_mmap_inner enforces no per-process or system-wide limit on mapped bytes (only the PROCESS_MMAP_START_VA..END_VA range), and mm/src/process_vm.rs:569 makes the mapping lazy, so an unprivileged loop of mmap+touch conve

**Fix.** Two separable pieces. (1) Break the freeing-requires-allocating cycle: the shootdown target list must come from a per-CPU preallocated buffer or a fixed bitmap (MAX_CPUS bits is 32 bytes, not 2 KiB) so that unmapping cannot fail under pressure — and if a remote flush truly cannot be issued, the correct degradation is to quarantine the frames, never to return Ok(()). (2) Give the buddy a reserve watermark that only kernel-context allocations may d

**Prior art.** Linux's ALLOC_WMARK_MIN/ALLOC_NO_WATERMARKS split exists exactly so the reclaim and unmap paths can allocate when userland cannot; PF_MEMALLOC and the mempool API exist so that the freeing path never depends on the allocating path; and the TLB batching code (tlb_gather_mmu) uses an on-stack fallback batch precisely bec

#### The reboot path never flushes the filesystem, and the ext2 image carries no dirty-state word, so every reboot silently discards write-back data onto an image that still says it is clean

*medium, confidence 90.* Two absences in two subsystems that are only dangerous together, with the seam owned by neither auditor. The fs auditor read ext2_vfs.rs and found a genuinely good ordered-writeback design with an LRU cache, a flusher kthread with congestion backoff, and a shutdown hook — and the module's own comment at :306 states that durability comes from "eviction + explicit sync + shutdown". The boot-arch auditor read shutdown.r

**Evidence.**
- boot/src/shutdown.rs:229-231 — `pub fn kernel_reboot(...) { ensure_shutdown_mmio_mapped(); cpu::disable_interrupts(); ...` — no call to flush_filesystems_for_shutdown(), and interrupts are off by line 231, so a flush could not complete even if added there.
- boot/src/shutdown.rs:138-145 — kernel_shutdown calls flush_filesystems_for_shutdown() first, with the comment "Doing this before `disable_interrupts` is load-bearing for durability; once interrupts are off a device flush would only time out."
- fs/src/ext2_vfs.rs:350-357 — ext2_vfs_shutdown_sync's doc: "Must be called while interrupts are still enabled (the virtio-blk completion path needs IRQs)". Its only caller is boot/src/shutdown.rs:36.

**Fix.** Call flush_filesystems_for_shutdown() as the first statement of kernel_reboot, before disable_interrupts, exactly as kernel_shutdown does — it is idempotent (guarded by the FS_SYNCED StateFlag at shutdown.rs:32). Separately, parse and maintain s_state: clear EXT2_VALID_FS at mount and set it in ext2_vfs_sync's final shutdown pass, so a crash or a lost reboot flush is visible to e2fsck instead of silently presenting a clean image. Without the seco

**Prior art.** Linux's reboot(2) path runs kernel_restart → kernel_restart_prepare → device_shutdown, and sys_reboot calls ksys_sync_helper() before any of it; the ext2 driver clears EXT2_VALID_FS in ext2_fill_super and restores it in ext2_put_super precisely so that an unclean shutdown is detectable. The combination SlopOS has — wri

#### The ext2 driver has no feature-compatibility gate and no read-only fallback: it mounts and writes any image whose magic and geometry are sane, including filesystems whose layout it cannot represent

*medium, confidence 85.* The fs auditor did excellent work on this exact file — SLOPOS-2026-0006 and #29/#30 are all about on-disk fields that are read and insufficiently *checked*. A field that is never read at all leaves no code to audit: s_feature_incompat, s_feature_ro_compat and s_feature_compat simply do not appear in the struct, so there is nothing to notice. Compounding it, the one place the missing feature bit has a visible effect —

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/ext2/ondisk.rs:33-79 — Superblock::parse reads 12 fields (magic, geometry, rev_level, inode_size) and validates only magic, non-zero divisors and block size. s_feature_compat (0x5C), s_feature_incompat (0x60) and s_feature_ro_compat (0x64) are never rea
- fs/src/ext2/dir.rs:42-43 — `let name_len = data[cursor + 6] as usize; let file_type = data[cursor + 7];` hardcodes the EXT2_FEATURE_INCOMPAT_FILETYPE layout; on an image without that feature, byte +7 is name_len's high byte and every entry's type reports as un
- fs/src/ext2/blockmap.rs + fs/src/ext2/inode.rs — the block map is the classic direct/indirect/double/triple scheme; an ext4 inode with EXT4_EXTENTS_FL set stores an extent tree in the same i_block array, which this code will read as block pointers and, on writ

**Fix.** Read the three feature words and apply the ext2 rule that exists for exactly this reason: refuse the mount if (s_feature_incompat & ~SUPPORTED_INCOMPAT) != 0; mount read-only if (s_feature_ro_compat & ~SUPPORTED_RO_COMPAT) != 0; ignore unknown compat bits. Declare SUPPORTED_INCOMPAT = FILETYPE and nothing else, which correctly rejects every ext3/ext4 image and makes the dir.rs:42 assumption sound instead of accidental. This is ~20 lines and it is

**Prior art.** ext2_fill_super in Linux has done exactly this since 1997 (EXT2_HAS_INCOMPAT_FEATURE → refuse; EXT2_HAS_RO_COMPAT_FEATURE → set MS_RDONLY), and the feature-flag triple was designed into the format specifically so that an old driver meets a new filesystem safely. Skipping it is how pre-feature-flag filesystems corrupted

#### No entropy accounting or reseeding: the CSPRNG's seed quality is unknowable to its consumers, and on a machine without RDRAND it is four rdtsc reads that getrandom() reports as success

*low, confidence 75.* The net auditor found the ISN's own weakness (#2/#173) and the DNS transaction-id weakness (#4) and correctly located them in net/. The absent mechanism is one layer down and in a different crate: there is a real ChaCha20 CSPRNG in drivers/, so any auditor who looked found a competent implementation and moved on. What is missing is not the generator but the *pool* — nothing accumulates entropy after boot, nothing res

**Evidence.**
- drivers/src/random.rs:8-12 — the module doc: seeded once at boot from "RDRAND (primary), RDSEED (bonus), and TSC (fallback)", then "auto-rekeys every 1 MB of output" — fast key erasure from its own keystream, which preserves but never increases entropy.
- boot/src/boot_drivers.rs:477-500 — boot_step_csprng_seed_fn tracks the source in a local `source` string for logging ("tsc" / "rdrand" / "rdrand+rdseed") and discards it; nothing durable records that the pool is TSC-only.
- drivers/src/random.rs:198-227 — emergency_seed()'s fallback fills 32 bytes from four rdtsc reads multiplied by fixed mixing constants. Consecutive rdtsc values at a fixed point in boot differ by a small, low-entropy delta, so the realistic entropy is a few ten

**Fix.** Record the seed provenance in a global (hardware / degraded) at boot, make getrandom(2) honour GRND_NONBLOCK by returning EAGAIN while the pool is degraded, and add a minimal entropy input path — interrupt timing jitter, or at least the first N device-completion timestamps — mixed in via a rekey so that a machine that boots without RDRAND converges to a usable pool rather than staying at its boot state forever. Also route mm/src/aslr.rs:90 throug

**Prior art.** Linux's random.c tracks entropy_count per source, blocks getrandom() until crng_ready(), prints the 'random: crng init done' line as an auditable event, and continuously mixes add_interrupt_randomness/add_input_randomness — the accounting exists specifically so a consumer can distinguish a seeded pool from a timestamp.

**Coverage verdict.** The 15-subsystem sweep is genuinely strong on *code that exists*: every parser, lock order, refcount and ABI struct in the tree got read by someone who understood it, and the four net criticals plus the mm TLB/LUF criticals are real. Its systematic blind spot is exactly what the lens predicts. (1) It cannot see a mechanism that was never written, so it reports the symptoms as independent bugs: "PROCESS_TLB_INFO is a 256-entry array indexed by an unbounded PID" (#124), "the pid allocator can wrap" (#48), "every fd syscall scans 256 slots" (#180), "input map is 16384 never-recycled task ids" (#192) are four sightings of one absent thing — a process identity object with a bounded, recycled id space — and nobody named the thing or checked the other index sites, one of which (page_fault.rs:13) kills the machine outright. (2) It audits each side of a seam against its own contract and never the composition: mm's demand-paging and CoW are correct as fault handlers, OSTD's validate_pages is correct as hardening, and the missing third thing — kernel-initiated fault-in — is nobody's file. (3) It trusts confident in-tree prose. `wait_queue.rs` states the signal-check discipline and names PipeReadOps::read as an exemplar; the auditors who read pipes and the auditors who read the signal machinery each believed the other side. Same for "durability comes from eviction + sync + shutdown" (reboot bypasses all three). What is still dark after both passes: crash-consistency of the on-disk image as a whole (no journal, no fs-state word, no feature gate, no sync on reboot — four absences that only matter together); the behaviour of the entire kernel below the memory watermark that does not exist (I traced one corruption path, there are certainly more, and #184's "506 fallible sites never executed" means nobody knows); and any end-to-end property that needs a running system to observe — there is no fuzzing, no fault injection, no soft-lockup detector and no benchmark (#241/#242/#252), so a whole class of absences remains structurally undiscoverable by reading, including by me.

*high, confidence 85.* The drivers-io auditor read `poll_rx` and checked what a *lying device* can do (finding 72, used-ring id masking) — the adversarial reading. **Evidence.**
- drivers/src/virtio_net.rs:330-343 — the NAPI poll path: `if let Some(new_page) = OwnedPageFrame::alloc_zeroed() { ...write_desc...; state.rx_buffers[idx] = Some(new_page); submit(idx) }` — with no `else`. On failure the slot stays `None` and the descriptor is 
- drivers/src/virtio_net.rs:983-993 — the identical pattern in the second RX drain path

#### `memfd_get_phys` discards the generation half of the handle the memfd registry exists to check, and the fd lookup that feeds it hands back a bare integer with the description reference already dropped

*medium, confidence 72.* The fs auditor verified that `FdSnapshot` holds a `KArc<OpenFile>` across every read/write — which is correct and is the property the fd table advertises. The mm auditor verified the memfd refcount/map_count protocol and the `MemfdHandle` generation scheme — also correct in isolation. The hole is in the one lookup helper that *drops* the snapshot before returning (`fileio_get_open_file_handle`) combined with the one 

**Evidence.**
- fs/src/fileio/fdops.rs:862-868 — `fileio_get_open_file_handle` builds an `FdSnapshot`, reads `kind` and `handle` out of it, and lets the `KArc<OpenFile>` drop at the end of the block. Callers get two integers and no reference
- fs/src/fileio/fdops.rs:870-873 — the sibling's doc states the shared assumption verbatim: "Confers no ownership: the caller's fd keeps the file alive for the duration of its own operation." With CLONE_VM threads sharing one fd table, "the caller's fd" can be c
- sched/src/task/task_lifecycle.rs:1431-1446 — `let share_vm = flags & CLONE_VM != 0;` … `let child_process_id = if share_vm { parent.process_id }` — threads share `process_id`, hence share the `process_id`-keyed fd table

**Fix.** Two independent fixes, both cheap. (1) Make `memfd_get_phys` generation-checking: resolve through the registry table (or publish a per-slot generation atomic alongside `MEMFD_PHYS` and compare it) so a stale packed handle returns NULL instead of another memfd's pages. (2) Change `fileio_get_open_file_handle` to return the `FdSnapshot` (or a `FileRef`) rather than a bare `usize`, so the description is pinned for the whole syscall — the type system

**Prior art.** io_uring's `io_file_get_normal`/`io_file_get_fixed` return an `io_kiocb`-owned `struct file *` with a reference taken under `files->file_lock`; no io_uring op ever carries a bare fd integer past the lookup. Fuchsia's zx_vmo handles are generation-tagged and every syscall resolves the handle to a `fbl::RefPtr<VmObject>`

**Coverage verdict.** The 253 findings are genuinely strong *within* each subsystem: net protocol hardening, fs on-disk parsing, ostd unsafe-surface soundness, sched lock discipline, and the build/verification honesty audit are all deep and mostly correct. What the per-subsystem partition systematically hid is the composition layer. Three specific darknesses remain. (1) **Nobody traced a syscall end-to-end.** `exec` is the clearest casualty: core/exec calls three mm entry points and assumes their union is "replace the address space"; mm's `process_vm_load_elf_data` unmaps exactly 4 MiB of code region and nothing else. The syscall-boundary auditor found that exec has no point-of-no-return (finding 57) — i.e. they read `do_exec` — and the mm auditor read `unmap_existing_code_region` — but neither asked what the *pair* accomplishes. (2) **Nobody owns the entry-context contract.** boot-arch knows #PF runs on IST4 with IF=0 and that IST stacks are not re-entrant (finding 106); mm knows `drain_by_phys_cross_cpu` requires and force-enables IRQs (finding 127). The composition — that the page-fault handler is a caller of the allocator — is stated nowhere and audited by no one. The same shape recurs anywhere an exception handler calls a subsystem written for process context. (3) **Object lifetime is expressed twice in incompatible vocabularies.** sched says a task is dead when its *status* is terminal; mm says an address space is dead when no CPU has its *CR3* loaded; fs says a description is alive while a `KArc` is held. Every pairwise translation between these is an unchecked assumption, and `destroy_process_vm` is where the sched→mm translation is simply wrong. Still dark after both sweeps: (a) no auditor exercised the SMP interleavings at all — every finding here and there is static reading, and the tree has no fault injection, no model checking and no concurrency tests (their finding 240), so the *real* distribution of these races is unmeasured; (b) the userland↔kernel protocol surface (slop-protocol wire format vs kernel compositor syscalls) was split across two auditors and neither traced a full client→compositor→kernel→scanout frame; (c) the ~15 small crates that map onto no audit area
